Eigenvarieties over CM fields and trianguline representations
Vaughan McDonald

Abstract
We show that the Galois representations associated to points on certain (derived) eigenvarieties for GL n \operatorname{GL}_{n} over a CM field are trianguline with the expected Sen weights, verifying an analogue of a conjecture of Hansen in many cases. The proof follows the strategy of passing to a larger unitary group G ~ \widetilde{G} of signature ( n , n ) (n,n) , where the key new input is an analytic continuation result for an eigenvariety for G ~ \widetilde{G} localised at an Eisenstein maximal ideal. We also discuss the (subtle) relation of eigenvarieties for GL n \operatorname{GL}_{n} with the trianguline variety.



1
Introduction

The purpose of this article is to clarify the relation between families of finite slope
p
-adic automorphic forms, or so-called
eigenvarieties
, and Galois representations. More precisely we show that points on certain eigenvarieties for the group
\operatorname{GL}_{n}
over a CM field first constructed by Fu in
[
Fu22b
]
have associated Galois representations which are
trianguline
at
p
, confirming an analogue of a conjecture of Hansen
[
Han17
, Conjecture 1.2.2]
.

To explain our result, we need some notation. See
Section

2.32
for more definitions and constructions. Let
F
be an imaginary CM field. Fix a sufficiently small tame level
K^{p}\subset\operatorname{GL}_{n}({\mathbb{A}}_{F}^{p,\infty})
, and let
{\mathbf{T}}(K^{p})
be a “big” spherical Hecke algebra as defined in
[
GN22
, 2.1.10]
. Let
\mathfrak{m}\subset{\mathbf{T}}(K^{p})
be a maximal ideal which is non-Eisenstein. For any
i\geq 0
we can construct from this data a rigid analytic space
\mathcal{E}^{i}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}
following
[
Fu22b
, §6]
, the eigenvariety for
\operatorname{GL}_{n}/F
of tame level
K^{p}
and cohomological degree
i
, localized at
\mathfrak{m}
. These spaces are a (derived) variant of Emerton’s eigenvarieties from
[
Eme06b
]
. The space
\mathcal{E}^{i}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}
is naturally a closed subspace of
(\operatorname{Spf}{\mathbf{T}}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\operatorname{red}})^{\operatorname{rig}}\times\widehat{T}
, where
\widehat{T}
is the rigid space of locally analytic characters of the diagonal maximal torus
T_{n}(F_{p})\subset\operatorname{GL}_{n}(F_{p})
, and
F_{p}:=F\otimes_{{\mathbf{Q}}}{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
. Scholze’s seminal work
[
Sch15
]
on torsion cohomology implies that there is a Galois representation
\rho:\operatorname{Gal}_{F}\to\operatorname{GL}_{n}({\mathbf{T}}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\operatorname{red}})
, which implies, for each point
(x,\delta)\in\mathcal{E}^{i}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}\subset(\operatorname{Spf}{\mathbf{T}}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\operatorname{red}})^{\operatorname{rig}}\times\widehat{T}
, there is an associated Galois representation
\rho_{x}:\operatorname{Gal}_{F}\to\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})
. First, we need to define a character
\rho_{\operatorname{cyc}}^{G}\in\widehat{T}
via the formula
(\rho_{\operatorname{cyc}}^{G})_{\nu}:=(1,\varepsilon_{\operatorname{cyc}}^{-1}\circ\operatorname{Art}_{F_{\nu}},\dots,\varepsilon_{\operatorname{cyc}}^{-1+n}\circ\operatorname{Art}_{F_{\nu}})
, where
\operatorname{Art}_{F_{\nu}}:F_{\nu}^{\times}\simeq W_{F_{\nu}}^{\operatorname{ab}}
is the local Artin map. Our main result then is as follows:

Theorem 1.1
.

Suppose
\mathfrak{m}\subset{\mathbf{T}}(K^{p})
is decomposed generic (in the sense of Caraiani–Scholze) and non-Eisenstein,
F
contains an imaginary quadratic field
F_{0}
, and
p
is a prime that splits in
F_{0}
. Then for any point
(x,\delta=(\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{n}))\in\mathcal{E}^{i}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}
and each place
\nu\mid p
of
F
, the associated Galois representation
\rho_{x}|_{\operatorname{Gal}_{F_{\nu}}}
is trianguline of parameter
(\delta+\rho_{\operatorname{cyc}}^{G})_{\nu}\cdot\delta^{\prime}
, where
\delta^{\prime}
is an algebraic character, and the
\tau
-Sen weights are
\{\operatorname{wt}_{\tau}(\delta_{1,\nu}),\operatorname{wt}_{\tau}(\delta_{2,\nu})+1,\dots,\operatorname{wt}_{\tau}(\delta_{n,\nu})+n-1\}
.

In essence, this result can be viewed as a
p
-adic (trianguline) local global compatibility result for
\operatorname{GL}_{n}
-eigenvarieties, which contextualizes recent progress in the Langlands program for CM fields in the framework of
p
-adic automorphic forms. A natural extension of
Theorem

1.1
would be to directly relate
\mathcal{E}^{i}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}
to triangulation deformation spaces as studied in
[
BHS17
,
BHS19
]
. See
Section

6.8
for more discussion of this topic.

1.2
History

Let us review the history of relating finite slope
p
-adic automorphic forms (or eigenvarieties) to trianguline Galois representations. For
\operatorname{GL}_{2}/{\mathbf{Q}}
, this link was first established by
[
Kis03
]
in the form of showing that if
f
is an overconvergent
p
-adic eigenform of finite slope, then
\rho_{f}
admits a crystalline period. Colmez
[
Col08
]
then crucially reinterpetted admitting crystalline periods in terms of being a
trianguline Galois representation
, i.e., the associated rigid analytic
(\varphi,\Gamma_{K})
-module being totally reducible, which he studied in detail in the
2
-dimensional case. For eigenvarieties for definite unitary groups of any dimension, triangulinity results were proved in many cases by
[
BC09
]
and later
[
Hel12
]
. These results were vastly generalized with the development of the theory of families of
(\varphi,\Gamma_{K})
-modules by Kedlaya–Pottharst–Xiao
[
KPX14
]
and independently Liu
[
Liu15
]
. In particular, their results imply there is a triangulinity result whenever the associated eigenvariety satisfies a suitable local-global compatibility for classical forms and the “classical points” are Zariski-dense (see
[
KPX14
, Theorem 6.3.13]
).

Simultaneously, the theory of eigenvarieties for groups without discrete series was being developed by various authors
[
Eme06b
,
AS08
,
Urb11
,
Han17
,
Fu22b
]
. Hansen
[
Han17
, Conjecture 1.2.2]
was the first to explicitly posit the connection between such eigenvarieties and trianguline representations. Our main result in essence proves his conjecture for the eigenvarieties of
[
Fu22b
]
under some assumptions on
F
, after localizing at certain maximal ideals. The main novelty in the result we prove is that one
does not expect
a Zariski-density of “classical points” for eigenvarieties for groups without discrete series, such as
\operatorname{GL}_{n}/F
for
n\geq 2
and
F
an imaginary CM field! See
[
CM09
, §1]
for further discussion of this issue.

1.3
Proof method

Let us discuss the proof strategy. We follow the lead of the 10 author paper
[
ACC+23
]
, which shows a local–global compatibility result in the ordinary case (see
[
ACC+23
, Theorem 5.5.1]
). Their work proceeds integrally, which presents some additional complications, but is also easier in certain steps due to the (relative) simplicity of the ordinary case.

As in
[
ACC+23
]
, we prove our local-global compatibility result by passing to a larger unitary group
\widetilde{G}=U(n,n)/F^{+}
, and considering its eigenvariety (in a fixed middle degree!). It is useful to briefly mention how our eigenvarieties are constructed. The spaces
\mathcal{E}^{i}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}
arise as the support of the Hecke algebra
{\mathbf{T}}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}
acting on a certain coherent sheaf
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{m}}^{i}
on the space
\widehat{T}
, whose global sections
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{m}}^{i}(\widehat{T})=(\mathrm{H}^{i}(J_{B_{n}}(\Pi(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\operatorname{la}})))^{\prime}
are the dual of a certain (derived version of) Emerton’s Jacquet module applied to (a complex computing) completed cohomology. The method of attack is to realize this coherent sheaf in the boundary cohomology of
\widetilde{G}
, and interpolate from the interior. Namely, we have eigenvarieties
\mathcal{E}^{d}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}},\mathcal{E}_{c}^{d}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}},\mathcal{E}_{\partial}^{i}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}
associated to the cohomology of
\widetilde{G}
, the compactly supported cohomology of
\widetilde{G}
, and the boundary cohomology of
\widetilde{G}
, respectively. These spaces arise as the Hecke module support of coherent sheaves
\mathcal{M}_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{c,\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{\partial,\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{i}
, which fit into a short exact sequence (see
Lemma

2.24
)

0\to\mathcal{M}_{\partial,\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{d}\to\mathcal{M}_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{d}\to\mathcal{M}_{c,\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{d}\to\mathcal{M}_{\partial,\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{d-1}\to 0.

(1)

With this sequence in mind, our work essentially consists of four steps:

1.

“
Degree shifting
:” relate the (co)homology
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{m}}^{i}
of
\operatorname{GL}_{n}/F
(in varying degrees
i
) to the boundary (co)homology
\mathcal{M}_{\partial,\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{d-1}
.

2.

Show the boundary (co)homology
\mathcal{M}_{\partial,\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{d-1}
is “small.”

3.

Show the middle degree (co)homology
\mathcal{M}_{c,\mathfrak{m}}^{d}
is “big” and then via Step 2 can be interpolated by classical
cuspidal
automorphic forms for
\widetilde{G}
.

4.

Use Steps 1 and 3 to deduce triangulinity results for
\operatorname{GL}_{n}/F
.

We now say more about each step. An important player in our arguments is the so-called
weight space

\mathcal{W}:=\widehat{T_{n}(\prod_{\nu\mid p}{\mathcal{O}}_{F_{\nu}})}
, the space of characters of the maximal compact torus
T_{0}\subset T
. The projection map
\widehat{T}\to\mathcal{W}
induces a natural map
\kappa:\mathcal{E}^{i}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}\to\mathcal{W}
, and similar maps for the other eigenvarieties for
\widetilde{G}
.

On the level of spherical Hecke algebras, the unitary group
\widetilde{G}
and
G:=\operatorname{GL}_{n}/F
are related via a Satake transfer map
\mathcal{S}:\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}\to{\mathbf{T}}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}
. Step 1 is then encapsulated in the following (see
Theorem

4.11
for a more precise statement):

Theorem 1.4
.

For all
i
the map
\mathcal{S}:\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}\to{\mathbf{T}}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}
induces a closed embedding
\mathcal{E}^{i}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{c}^{d}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}
fitting into a diagram

{}{}}}{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-11.1327pt}{-15.52419pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -15.4 -21.48)} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

where
w\in W^{\overline{P}}
(see
Section

3
for a definition) satisfies
\ell(w)=d-1-i
, and the bottom map denotes the dot action
\lambda\mapsto w^{-1}\cdot_{\operatorname{alg}}\lambda:=w^{-1}(\lambda+w_{0}^{\widetilde{G}}\rho^{\widetilde{G}})-w_{0}^{\widetilde{G}}\rho^{\widetilde{G}}
, where
\rho^{\widetilde{G}}
is the half sum of positive roots for
\widetilde{G}
, and
w_{0}^{\widetilde{G}}
is the long element of the Weyl group of
\widetilde{G}
.

The proof consists of a computation of (a piece of) the nilpotent Lie algebra cohomology of a certain parabolic induction of a complex.

Step 2 can be summarized as the following (see
Corollary

2.31
for notation):

Theorem 1.5
.

There is an admissible affinoid open cover
\bigcup_{i}U_{i}=\operatorname{supp}_{\mathcal{W}\times{\mathbf{G}}_{m}}J_{B}^{\vee}(C_{\bullet}^{\partial,\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I,D(I)))\subset\mathcal{W}\times{\mathbf{G}}_{m}
such the image
\pi_{\mathcal{W}}=:W_{i}\subset\mathcal{W}
under the map
\pi_{\mathcal{W}}:\mathcal{W}\times{\mathbf{G}}_{m}\to\mathcal{W}
is an affinoid open, and for all
j\geq 0
,
M_{\partial,\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{j}(U_{i})
is a finitely generated
torsion

\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}(W_{i})
-module.

This result is not entirely new, as it follows the strategy of
[
Han17
, §4]
, who explained how to prove such a result for
overconvergent cohomology
. In fact, our proof strategy is to adapt the tools Hansen uses (that is, technical tools about slope decompositions from
[
AS08
]
and
[
Urb11
]
) to our setting. The key point is that the
classical
(cuspidal) cohomology of
\operatorname{GL}_{n}
is only non-zero for certain weights
\lambda
, which live in a proper subvariety of weight space. A similar fact holds true for boundary cohomology. Then using a small slope classicality result, we can show many fibres of this boundary Jacquet module are zero, which shows torsion-ness.

For Step 3, we can phrase everything in terms of the middle degree eigenvariety
\mathcal{E}_{c}^{d}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}
:

Theorem 1.6
.

The eigenvariety
\mathcal{E}_{c}^{d}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}
is equidimensional of dimension
\dim\mathcal{W}=2n[F^{+}:{\mathbf{Q}}]
with no embedded components, and the image of an irreducible component of
\mathcal{E}_{c}^{d}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}
under the weight map
\widetilde{\kappa}
is Zariski open in
\mathcal{W}
.
Moreover,
\mathcal{E}_{c}^{d}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}
contains a Zariski-dense and accumulating set of points corresponding to classical cohomological cuspidal automorphic representations of
\widetilde{G}({\mathbb{A}}_{F^{+}})
.

We note that in contrast to the torsion boundary
\mathcal{M}_{\partial,\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{d-1}
, this middle degree unitary group (co)homology
\mathcal{M}_{c,\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{d}
will turn out to be locally
torsion-free
over weight space! On the other hand, the fact that
\mathcal{M}_{\partial,\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{d-1}
is
torsion
is absolutely crucial to the Zariski-density of classical cuspidal points in
Theorem

1.6
. The reason is that while
\mathcal{E}^{d}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}
may not be equidimensional, it coincides with the usual eigenvariety constructed by Emerton, and thus many classical points contribute. We hope this strategy might be helpful to study other properties of eigenvarieties for groups without discrete series.

Remark 1.7

(Completed versus overconvergent cohomology)
.

A natural question is whether our results can be applied to other eigenvarieties constructed in the literature. For example, Hansen’s original conjecture about triangulinity is for eigenvarieties coming from overconvergent cohomology. As is written, the only Step for which our argument seems to strongly use Jacquet functors is the “degree shifting” in Step 1. For partial progress of a degree-shifting nature in a more overconvergent context, see
[
Fu22a
]
.

While we do not pursue directly comparing eigenvarieties from completed and overconvergent (co)homology, we hope our results highlight the utility of using different features of the various formulations of eigenvarieties. Moreover, we mention ongoing work of Johansson–Tarrach which aims to compare (versions of) these two theories using Tarrach’s theory of so-called
p
-arithmetic (co)homology
[
Tar23
]
.

Remark 1.8
.

Much of this paper requires doing homological algebra with locally analytic representations on LB or Fréchet spaces, which presents annoying issues. Naturally, our saving grace is that eigenvarieties are constructed from
coadmissible
modules over certain Fréchet algebras, which form a nice abelian category where objects have canonical topologies and all maps are automatically strict.

One might wonder or expect that the notion of a “derived eigenvariety” should most naturally approached using the recent theory of condensed mathematics, specifically via the solid locally analytic representation theory developed in
[
RJRC22
,
RJRC23
]
. The current draft opts to work in more classical language, partially out of ignorance, and also because we feel it is useful to delineate where such theoretical advantages are truly necessary for proofs. As such, we do not use the theory of solid modules to run our arguments.

Nevertheless, for future applications it might be convenient to recast parts of our work in the solid language. We hope to return to this in the near future.

1.9
Related work for CM fields

We now discuss the relation between our work and the many recent works on proving
p
-adic Hodge theoretic properties (i.e. local-global compatibility at
\ell=p
) of Galois representations attached to (cohomological cuspidal) Hecke eigensystems for
\operatorname{GL}_{n}
over a CM field. For any
n>2
the only known method for constructing Galois representations associated to Hecke eigenclasses for
\operatorname{GL}_{n}/F
comes by passing to the
2n
-variable unitary group
\widetilde{G}
. This strategy was first realised by
[
HLTT16
]
for characteristic
0
eigenclasses, and then also by Scholze
[
Sch15
]
for torsion classes. In fact, the construction in
[
HLTT16
]
realises
R_{\pi}\oplus R_{\pi}^{c,\vee}
(twisted) as a limit of
finite slope
cohomology classes on the ordinary locus of the Shimura variety for
\widetilde{G}
.
1
1

1

In fact, it seems that Skinner was the first to envision such a strategy, and had in mind to use eigenvarieties for these constructions.
Although congruences are not sufficient for proving
p
-adic Hodge theoretic properties, this fact perhaps makes our work seem more plausible.

Since then (due to motivations from the Calegari–Geraghty method for proving automorphy results) there has been much recent work on proving such properties. With the crucial input from Caraiani–Scholze’s work on torsion vanishing
[
CS19
]
,
[
ACC+23
, §4, §5]
developed a robust strategy from local-global compatibility via so-called “degree–shifting arguments.” We briefly mention works which pursue this strategy: all under various technical assumptions,
[
ACC+23
, Theorem 4.5.1, Theorem 5.5.1]
handle the Fontaine–Laffaille and ordinary cases,
[
A’C24
, Theorem 4.3.3]
handles a rather general case in characteristic
0
(via ideas of Caraiani–Newton),
[
CN23
]
proves the crystalline torsion case, and finally
[
Hev23
]
proves the general potentially semistable torsion case. We note that these last two works must assume
F
is not imaginary quadratic, whereas
[
ACC+23
, Theorem 5.5.1]
and our work need no such assumption.

1.10
What our work does

Our work uses
[
CS19
]
in an essential way (for example, to get (
1
)), and is the reason for the “decomposed generic” assumption in
Theorem

1.1
. We also use
[
Sch15
]
to get the Galois representation
\rho
on
\mathcal{E}^{i}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}
, although we likely could use our method to independently reconstruct
\rho
. Otherwise, our work does not rely on the further developments mentioned above.

As previously mentioned, our argument most closely mirrors the ordinary case of
[
ACC+23
, §5]
. The key new inputs in our case are a degree shifting argument in the locally analytic context (where functions on Bruhat strata are more complicated) (Step 1), and the analytic continuation result for
\mathcal{E}_{c}^{d}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}
(Step 3). While many of the arguments come via standard methods in theory of eigenvarieties, the overall strategy to combine these methods seems to not have been exploited before.

We note another technical difference concerning the unitary group
\widetilde{G}
. The authors in
loc. cit.
need to choose certain dominant weights for
\widetilde{G}
very carefully, and then appeal to a trick with the centre to handle all possible degrees of cohomology for
\operatorname{GL}_{n}/F
. Our general analytic continuation result is much softer, and does not require explicit care in choosing weights only contributing to the interior.

1.11
Contents

We now review the contents of the article.
Section

2
reviews preliminaries on the (co)homology of locally symmetric spaces, recalls the derived Jacquet functors of
[
Fu22b
]
, and shows two key properties: the sequence (
1
) and
Theorem

1.5
.
Section

3
discusses some constructions related to parabolic inductions and filtrations by Bruhat strata. Then
Section

4
uses Lie algebra cohomology to relate the derived Jacquet functors for
\operatorname{GL}_{n}
(of varying degrees) to a derived Jacquet module for
\widetilde{G}
(in middle degree). This relation proves
Theorem

1.4
.
Section

5
then establishes the necessary results about unitary group in middle degree, proving
Theorem

1.6
. Lastly,
Section

6
combines the previous work to prove
Theorem

1.1
, and then discusses some further questions about trianguline deformations.

We thank Lie Qian for inspiring discussions in the early stages of this paper and making us appreciate Lie algebra cohomology, as well as for various conversations afterwards. We thank Weibo Fu for laying the foundations that make this work possible, and for a discussion early on about his impressions on eigenvarieties. We thank Christian Johansson for some very helpful discussions about comparing eigenvarieties, overconvergent homology, and especially helping with
Lemma

2.24
. We thank Johansson and also Brian Conrad for pointers regarding
Lemma

6.4
. Lastly, we thank Eugen Hellmann for many discussions and his continued interest in our work. We also thank Sean Cotner, Matt Emerton, Dongryul Kim, and James Newton for helpful discussions. This research was conducted while the author was an NSF Graduate Fellow, and we warmly acknowledge their support.

1.12
Notation and conventions

For
F/{\mathbf{Q}}
be a number field let
S_{p}(F)
denote the set of
p
-adic places in
F
. For any finite place
\nu\in S_{p}(F)
, we let
F_{\nu}
denote the associated complete local field, a finite extension of
{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
. Let
\mathcal{O}_{F_{\nu}}\subset F_{\nu}
its ring of integers,
\mathfrak{m}_{\nu}\subset\mathcal{O}_{F_{\nu}}
is maximal ideal, and
k_{\nu}:=\mathcal{O}_{F_{\nu}}/\mathfrak{m}_{\nu}
the residue field. We will often let
\varpi_{\nu}\in\mathfrak{m}_{F_{\nu}}
denote a uniformizer. We denote
q_{\nu}:=\#k_{\nu}
. Let
F_{p}:=F\otimes_{{\mathbf{Q}}}{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
. Note we have a decomposition
F_{p}\simeq\prod_{\nu\in S_{p}(F)}F_{\nu}
. Similarly, define
\mathcal{O}_{F,p}:=\mathcal{O}_{F}\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}}{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}
.

If
R
is a ring, let
\mathsf{D}(R)
denote the derived category of
R
-modules, and
\mathsf{K}(R)
the homotopy category of complexes of
R
-modules.

Let
L/{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
be a finite extension. If
V
is a locally convex
L
-vector space, we let
V^{\prime}
denote its strong dual.
Recall that a locally convex topological
L
-vector space
V
is of
compact type
if it can be written as an inductive limit
V\simeq\varinjlim_{n}V_{n}
for
\{V_{n}\}
a countable inductive system of
L
-Banach spaces with compact, injective transition maps, where
V
is endowed with the inductive limit topology.
These spaces are naturally reflexive, and
V\mapsto V^{\prime}
provides an anti-equivalence between compact type spaces and so-called nuclear Fréchet spaces over
L
(see
[
ST02b
, Theorem 1.3]
).

Given a complex
C^{\bullet}
of locally convex topological
L
-vector spaces with continuous differentials, we often topologize
\mathrm{H}^{\ast}(C^{\bullet})
with the induced subquotient topology. Note these spaces need not be Hausdorff.

Let
H
be a compact
p
-adic Lie group. Then we denote
{\mathcal{O}}_{L}[[H]]:=\varinjlim_{m\geq 0,H^{\prime}\lhd H}{\mathcal{O}}_{L}/p^{m}[H/H^{\prime}]
, which is naturally a noetherian ring. We also let
L[[H]]:={\mathcal{O}}_{L}[[H]]\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{L}}L
.
Let
G
be a general locally
{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
-analytic group. Similarly, we let
\mathcal{C}(G,L)
denote the space of continuous functions from
G\to L
, and
\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{la}}(G,L)
the subspace of locally analytic functions. For
H
compact,
\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{la}}(H,L)
is naturally a space of compact type. We let
\mathsf{Ban}_{L}^{\operatorname{ad}}(G)
denote the category of admissible Banach representations of
G
, and
\operatorname{Rep}_{\rm{la.c}}(G)
the category of locally representations of
G
on compact type vector spaces (see
[
Eme06a
, §3.1]
). Let be
H_{1}\supset H_{2}

p
-adic Lie groups such that
H_{1}/H_{2}
is compact. Then if
\pi
is Banach representation of
H_{2}
we let
\operatorname{ct}-\operatorname{Ind}_{H_{2}}^{H_{1}}\pi
be the continuous induction, the set of continuous functions
H_{1}\to\pi
such that
f(h_{2}g)=\pi(h_{2})f(g)
for
h_{2}\in H_{2}
. Similarly if
\pi\in\operatorname{Rep}_{\rm{la.c}}(H_{2}),
we denote
\operatorname{Ind}_{H_{2}}^{H_{1}}\pi
the locally analytic induction defined via the same equivariance.

Similarly, let
D(G,L):=(\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{la}}(G,L))^{\prime}
be the distribution algebra associated to
G
. When
G
is compact, this is naturally a nuclear
L
-Fréchet algebra. Suppose also that
G
is a compact, uniform pro-
p
group. For any
1/p<r<1
, we let
D_{r}(G,L)
and
D_{<r}(G,L)
be the
L
-Banach spaces defined in
[
ST03
, §4]
. For any
1/p\leq r^{\prime}<r<1
we have embeddings
D_{r}(G,L)\hookrightarrow D_{<r}(G,L)\hookrightarrow D_{r^{\prime}}(G,L)
. Moreover if
r\in p^{{\mathbf{Q}}}
then the maps
L[[G]]\hookrightarrow D_{r}(G,L)\hookrightarrow D_{<r}(G,L)
are flat, and all three algebras are (left and right) noetherian integral domains by
[
ST03
, §4]
. Lastly, the distribution algebra is a limit
D(G,L)=\varprojlim_{r}D_{r}(G,L)=\varprojlim_{r}D_{<r}(G,L)
.
If
H
is in fact an abelian p-adic Lie group, we let
\widehat{H}
(the space of
characters of
H
) be the rigid space over
{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
representing the functor
\operatorname{Sp}(A)\mapsto\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cont}}(H,A^{\times})
. See
[
Eme17
, §6.4]
for more details.

If
G
is a split connected reductive group with maximal torus
T\subset G
, denote by
W_{G}
the associated Weyl group. Let
w_{0}^{G}
denote the long element. Let
X^{\ast}(T)
be the character lattice. If we also choose a Borel
B\supset T
, we can define a notion of positive roots, and then form
\rho^{G}\in X^{\ast}(T)
, the half sum of positive roots. For any
w\in W_{G}
, we may define the dot action
w\cdot\lambda:=w(\lambda+\rho^{G})-\rho^{G}
.

Let
K/{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
be a finite extension. We take the geometric normalisation of local class field theory by taking the Artin map
\operatorname{Art}_{K}:K^{\times}\xrightarrow{\sim}W_{K}^{\rm{ab}}
to send uniformizers to geometric Frobenius. Let
\operatorname{rec}_{K}
be the local Langlands correspondence as normalised in
[
HT01
]
. We denote the cyclotomic character by
\varepsilon_{\operatorname{cyc}}:\operatorname{Gal}_{K}\to\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}^{\times}
. Let
\chi:K^{\times}\to\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}^{\times}
be a continuous (and thus locally
{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
-analytic) character. We then define its
weight
to be
\operatorname{wt}(\chi)=(\operatorname{wt}_{\tau}(\chi))_{\tau\in\operatorname{Hom}(K,\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})}\in K\otimes_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}
by
\operatorname{wt}(\chi):=-\frac{d}{dt}\chi(\exp(tx))|_{t=0}\in\operatorname{Hom}(K,{\mathbf{Q}}_{p})\otimes_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}\simeq K\otimes_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}
where the identification
\operatorname{Hom}(K,{\mathbf{Q}}_{p})\simeq K
is via the trace pairing. For any
\underline{k}=(k_{\tau})_{\tau}\in{\mathbf{Z}}^{\operatorname{Hom}(K,\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})}
, we define the character
z^{\underline{k}}:K^{\times}\to\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}^{\times}
via the formula
x\mapsto\prod_{\tau\in\operatorname{Hom}(K,\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})}(\tau(x))^{k_{\tau}}
. By construction,
\operatorname{wt}(x^{\underline{k}})=-\underline{k}
. We call such characters
algebraic
. We normalise Hodge-Tate(-Sen) weights so that
\operatorname{HT}(\varepsilon_{\operatorname{cyc}})=-1
.
We let
\mathcal{R}_{K}
be the Robba ring for
K
and we denote
R_{L,K}:=L\otimes_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}\mathcal{R}_{K}
(see
[
KPX14
, §2.2]
).

2
Preliminaries on (co)homology

In this section we collect preliminaries of various sorts about (co)homology, Jacquet functors, and Hecke algebras. Part of this section builds on the foundations from
[
Fu22b
]
by studying derived Jacquet functors on the homology side. The key results we will need in the sequel are
Lemma

2.24
(sequence (
1
)) and
Corollary

2.31
(
Theorem

1.5
).

2.1
Locally symmetric spaces and completed (co)homology

We follow the setup of
[
ACC+23
, Section 2]
.
Suppose
F
is a number field and
G
is (an
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-model of) a connected linear algebraic group over
F
. Let
X^{G}
be the symmetric space for
\operatorname{Res}_{F/{\mathbf{Q}}}G
, a homogeneous space for
G(F\otimes_{{\mathbf{Q}}}{\mathbf{R}})
(see
[
BS73
, Section 2]
or
[
NT16
, 3.1]
).

Recall a compact open subgroup
K\subset G({\mathbb{A}}_{F}^{\infty})
is
good
if it is neat and of the form
\prod_{\nu}K_{\nu}
where
\nu
runs over finite places of
F
. Let
K^{p}=\prod_{\nu\nmid p}K_{\nu}
. We then consider the quotients

X_{K}^{G}:=G(F)\backslash(X^{G}\times G({\mathbb{A}}_{F}^{\infty})/K),\quad X_{K^{p}}^{G}:=G(F)\backslash(X^{G}\times G({\mathbb{A}}_{F}^{\infty})/K^{p}),

where for the latter space we give
G({\mathbb{A}}_{F}^{\infty})
the discrete topology. These spaces then both have the structure of smooth manifolds, and the projection map
\pi_{K_{p}}:X_{K^{p}}^{G}\to X_{K}^{G}
induces an identification
X_{K}^{G}=X_{K^{p}}^{G}/K_{p}
.

We likewise let
\overline{X}^{G}
be the partial Borel–Serre compactification of
X^{G}
as in
[
BS73
, 7.1]
. Defining

\overline{X}_{K}^{G}:=G(F)\backslash(\overline{X}^{G}\times G({\mathbb{A}}_{F}^{\infty})/K),\quad\overline{X}_{K^{p}}^{G}:=G(F)\backslash(\overline{X}^{G}\times G({\mathbb{A}}_{F}^{\infty})/K^{p}),

we again have an identification
\overline{X}_{K}^{G}=\overline{X}_{K^{p}}^{G}/K_{p}
, and in fact
\overline{X}_{K}^{G}
is a compact smooth manifold with corners with interior
X_{K}^{G}
. Additionally, the inclusion
X_{K}^{G}\hookrightarrow\overline{X}_{K}^{G}
is a homotopy equivalence. Such an equivalence is induced by a homotopy inverse
p:\overline{X}^{G}\to X^{G}
. In particular, this map
p
gives us homotopy inverses
p_{K^{p}}:\overline{X}_{K^{p}}^{G}\to X_{K^{p}}^{G}
and
p_{K}:\overline{X}_{K}^{G}\to X_{K}^{G}
so that
p_{K^{p}}
is
G_{p}:=G(F_{p})
-equivariant and upon quotienting by
K_{p}
induces
p_{K}
.

We also define
\partial X^{G}:=\overline{X}^{G}-X^{G}
and using this space we define
\partial X_{K}^{G}
and
\partial X_{K^{p}}^{G}
via analogous double quotients. For
K^{\prime}\subset K\subset G({\mathbb{A}}_{F}^{\infty})
good compact open subgroups we then have diagrams

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

(2)

We also have analogous diagrams for
X_{K^{p}}^{G}
and the boundary spaces, and these diagrams are also compatible with the projection maps
\pi_{K_{p}}
.

We are interested in the (co)homology of
X_{K}^{G},\partial X_{K}^{G}
, in various guises. For any topological space
M
we let
C_{\bullet}(M)
denote the complex of singular chains. Note then that
C_{\bullet}(X_{K^{p}}^{G})
carries a right action of
G_{p}:=G(F_{p})
by right translation. Moreover, if
K_{p}\subset G_{p}
is a compact open subgroup such that
K^{p}K_{p}
is good, then
C_{\bullet}(X_{K^{p}}^{G})
is in fact a complex of free
{\mathbf{Z}}[K_{p}]
-modules. For any left
{\mathbf{Z}}[K_{p}]
-module
M
, we let
C_{\bullet}(K^{p}K_{p},M):=C_{\bullet}(X_{K^{p}}^{G})\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}[K_{p}]}M
, the homology complex with coefficients
M
.

For the purposes of p-adic automorphic forms, it is important to represent
C_{\bullet}(X_{K^{p}}^{G})
by complexes with better finiteness properties. Not surprisingly, we essentially use simplicial homology.
Fix
K^{p}
, and let
K_{p}\subset G_{p}
such that
K=K^{p}K_{p}\subset G({\mathbb{A}}_{F}^{\infty})
is a good subgroup.
Now fix a finite triangulation
\tau
of
\overline{X}_{K^{p}K_{p}}^{G}
, chosen so that
\partial\overline{X}_{K^{p}K_{p}}^{G}
occurs as a subtriangulation. Let
T_{n}^{\circ}(X_{K}^{G})
be the set of simplices of dimension
n
occurring in
\tau
. Let
T_{n}^{\circ}(X_{K^{p}}^{G})
be the set of simplices lying above those in
T_{n}^{\circ}(X_{K}^{G})
. Further fixing a simplex
\Delta\in T_{n}^{\circ}(X_{K}^{G})
, let
T_{n}^{\circ}(X_{K^{p}}^{G})/\Delta
be the set of simplices in
\overline{X}_{K^{p}}
lying over
\Delta
.

We then define
C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}):=\bigoplus_{\Delta\in T_{\bullet}^{\circ}(X_{K^{p}}^{G})}{\mathbf{Z}}\cdot\Delta
. Since the projection map
\pi_{K_{p}}:\overline{X}_{K^{p}}^{G}\to\overline{X}_{K^{p}K_{p}}^{G}
is a
K_{p}
-torsor (and thus a covering map since we are endowing
K_{p}
with the discrete topology), by the homotopy lifting lemma we have that any
\Delta\in T_{n}^{\circ}(X_{K^{p}K_{p}}^{G})
has a lift to
\overline{X}_{K^{p}}^{G}
. Choosing such lifts, we may rewrite

C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p})=\bigoplus_{\Delta\in T_{\bullet}^{\circ}(X_{K}^{G})}\bigoplus_{\Delta^{\prime}:\pi_{K_{p}}(\Delta^{\prime})=\Delta}{\mathbf{Z}}\simeq\bigoplus_{\Delta\in T_{\bullet}^{\circ}(X_{K}^{G})}{\mathbf{Z}}[K_{p}]
, so that
C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p})
is in fact a bounded complex of free
{\mathbf{Z}}[K_{p}]
-modules.

By the equivalence of simplicial and singular homology, the natural
{\mathbf{Z}}[K_{p}]
-linear map

\iota_{\tau}:C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p})\to C_{\bullet}(K^{p})

is in fact a homotopy equivalence of complexes of
{\mathbf{Z}}
-modules. But in fact,
[
Wei94
, Corollary 10.4.7]
(using that
C_{\bullet}(K^{p})
is a bounded
above
complex of free
{\mathbf{Z}}[K_{p}]
-modules), we may choose a
{\mathbf{Z}}[K_{p}]
-linear homotopy inverse
p_{\tau}:C_{\bullet}(K^{p})\to C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K)
. Likewise, let
C_{\bullet}^{\partial}(K^{p}):=C_{\bullet}(\partial X_{K^{p}}^{G})
be the complex of singular chains for the boundary of the Borel–Serre compactification. We can then define
C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BM}}(K^{p})
to be the cone of the natural inclusion

0\to C_{\bullet}^{\partial}(K^{p})\to C_{\bullet}(K^{p})\to C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BM}}(K^{p})\to 0.

Using our choice
\tau
of finite triangulation of
\overline{X}_{K^{p}K_{p}}^{G}
which has
\partial X_{K^{p}K_{p}}^{G}
as a finite subtriangulation, we can define an analogous chain complex
C_{\bullet}^{\partial,\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p})
of finite free
{\mathbf{Z}}[K_{p}]
-modules with a
{\mathbf{Z}}[K_{p}]
-linear homotopy equivalence
C_{\bullet}^{\partial,\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p})\xrightarrow{\iota_{\partial}}C_{\bullet}^{\partial}(K^{p})
. We may likewise define
C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BM},\operatorname{BS}}(K)
to be the cone of the map
C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K)\to C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K)
. For any set of decorations
\ast=\emptyset,\partial,\operatorname{BS},\operatorname{BM},(\operatorname{BM},\operatorname{BS}),(\partial,\operatorname{BS})
and any left
K_{p}
-module
M
, we define
C_{\bullet}^{\ast}(K^{p}K_{p},M):=C_{\bullet}^{\ast}(K^{p})\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}[K_{p}]}M
. We then have a commuting diagram of complexes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\bullet}^{\partial}(K^{p}K_{p},M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\bullet}(K^{p}K_{p},M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\bullet}(K^{p}K_{p},M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\bullet}^{\partial,\operatorname{BS}}(K,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}K_{p},M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{C}_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BM},\operatorname{BS}}(K,M)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\tau,\partial}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\operatorname{BS}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\operatorname{BS}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\tau}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\tau,\operatorname{BM}}}

where
\iota_{\tau,\partial},\iota_{\tau},\iota_{\tau,\operatorname{BM}}
are each quasi-isomorphisms. Again, we can use
[
Wei94
, Corollary 10.4.7]
to form homotopy inverses
p_{\tau,\partial},p_{\tau,\operatorname{BM}}
. In the sequel will usually suppress the triangulation
\tau
from the notation, even though these homotopy equivalences depend on such a choice.

Remark 2.2
.

Unfortunately, it is not clear that
p_{\tau,\partial},p_{\tau}
in general can be chosen to be compatible on the nose, i.e. that
f^{\operatorname{BS}}\circ p_{\tau,\partial}=p_{\tau}\circ f
. Of course, these compositions induce the same map on homology.

2.2.1
Completed (co)homology

Let
L/{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
be a finite extension. Recall completed homology and cohomology are defined by

\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}_{\ast}(K^{p},{\mathcal{O}}_{L}):=\varprojlim_{K_{p}\to 1}\mathrm{H}_{\ast}(X_{K^{p}K_{p}}^{G},{\mathcal{O}}_{L}),\quad\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}^{\ast}(K^{p},{\mathcal{O}}_{L}):=\varprojlim_{n}\varinjlim_{K_{p}\to 1}\mathrm{H}^{\ast}(X_{K^{p}K_{p}}^{G},{\mathcal{O}}_{L}/p^{n}).

Likewise, we let
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}_{\ast}(K^{p},L):=\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}_{\ast}(K^{p},{\mathcal{O}}_{L})\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{L}}L,\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}^{\ast}(K^{p},L):=\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}^{\ast}(K^{p},{\mathcal{O}}_{L})\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{L}}L
.
The former groups
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}_{\ast}(K^{p},{\mathcal{O}}_{L})
are finitely generated modules over
{\mathcal{O}}_{L}[[K_{p}]]
, while the latter
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}^{\ast}(K^{p},L)
are admissible Banach space representations of
G(F_{p})
. Recall that
[
CE12
, Theorem 1.1 (3)]
implies
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}^{\ast}(K^{p},L)\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{cont}}(\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}_{\ast}(K^{p},L),L)
are dual under the natural anti-equivalence between finitely generated
L[[K_{p}]]
-modules and admissible Banach
K_{p}
-representations (see
[
ST02a
, Theorem 3.5]
).
Connecting to our previous definitions, a basic fact is that
\mathrm{H}_{\ast}(C(K^{p}K_{p},{\mathcal{O}}_{L}[[K_{p}]]))\simeq\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}_{\ast}(K_{p},{\mathcal{O}}_{L})
(see
[
JNWE24
, Proposition 6.3.5]
), and thus
C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K,{\mathcal{O}}_{L}[[K_{p}]])
is a complex of finite free
{\mathcal{O}}_{L}[[K_{p}]]
-modules whose homology computes
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}_{\ast}(K^{p},{\mathcal{O}}_{L})
. Analogous statements hold true for boundary and Borel–Moore homology.

We may also consider the space
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}^{\ast}(K^{p},L)^{\operatorname{la}}
of locally
{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
-analytic vectors. For any locally
{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
-analytic group
H
, write for short
D(H)=D(H,L)=(\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{la}}(H,L))^{\prime}
. Recall then by
[
ST03
, Proof of Prop 7.1]
that if
V
is an admissible Banach representation of
H
then
(V^{\operatorname{la}})^{\prime}=D(H)\otimes_{L[[H]]}V^{\prime}
, and moreover
D(H)
is faithfully flat over
L[[H]]
. These results immediately imply that the complex
C^{\operatorname{BS}}_{\bullet}(K,D(K_{p}))
computes the dual of locally analytic completed cohomology
\left(\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}^{\ast}(K^{p},L)^{\operatorname{la}}\right)^{\prime}
.

2.2.2
Complexes for completed cohomology

We also needs nice complexes computing cohomology. While one might expect that nice complexes (at least rationally) will just follow from taking the dual of the picture just described, there are potentially issues in dualizing the larger complex
C(K^{p},L[[K_{p}]])
, which would make it harder to define an action of
G(F_{p})
up to homotopy. So instead we will recall the setup from
[
Fu22b
]
.

Continue to take
K^{p}K_{p}\subset G({\mathbb{A}}_{F}^{\infty})
to be good.
Fix a countable basis of open normal subgroups of
K_{p}
, denoted by
(K_{p}^{r})_{r=1}^{\infty}
, and set
X_{r}:=X_{K^{p}K_{p}^{r}}^{G},\overline{X}_{r}:=\overline{X}_{K^{p}K_{p}^{r}}^{G}
. Then we have a tower of maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{X}_{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{X}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{X}_{0}.}

Fixing again a triangulation
\tau
of
\overline{X}_{0}
, let
T_{n}(X_{0})
be the set of all singular
n
-simplices of
\overline{X}_{0}
, we let
T_{n}^{\circ}(X_{0})
and the set of
n
-simplices occurring in
\tau
. As in
Section

2.1
, we likewise define
T_{n}(X_{r})
,
T_{n}^{\circ}(X_{r})
,
T_{n}^{\circ}(X_{r})/\Delta
. Then look at the singular (co)chain complexes

\displaystyle A_{r,s}^{n}:=\prod_{\Delta\in T_{n}(X_{r})}\operatorname{Hom}\left({\mathbf{Z}}\Delta,\mathcal{O}_{L}/p^{s}\right)=\operatorname{Hom}(\bigoplus_{\Delta\in T_{n}(X_{r})}{\mathbf{Z}}\Delta,\mathcal{O}_{L}/p^{s})

\displaystyle\Pi_{r,s}^{n}:=\bigoplus_{\Delta\in T_{n}^{\circ}(X_{r})}\operatorname{Hom}({\mathbf{Z}}\Delta,\mathcal{O}_{L}/p^{s}),

As before, we can choose a
{\mathbf{Z}}[K_{p}]
-equivariant homotopy inverse
p
of the inclusion
i:C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p})=\bigoplus_{T_{\bullet}^{\circ}(X_{K^{p}}^{G})}{\mathbf{Z}}\to\bigoplus_{T_{\bullet}(X_{K^{p}}^{G})}{\mathbf{Z}}=C(K^{p})
. Such a map induces
{\mathbf{Z}}[K_{p}/K_{p}^{r}]
-linear homotopy inverses to the inclusions
\bigoplus_{T_{\bullet}^{\circ}(X_{r})}{\mathbf{Z}}\to\bigoplus_{T_{\bullet}(X_{r})}{\mathbf{Z}}
.
Such inverses give us compatible
K_{p}/K_{p}^{r}
-linear homotopy inverses to the
K_{p}/K_{p}^{r}
-equivariant quasi-isomorphisms,
A_{r,s}^{\bullet}\to\Pi_{r,s}^{\bullet}
. Then upon setting
A^{\bullet}:=\varprojlim_{s}\varinjlim_{r}A_{r,s},\Pi^{\bullet}:=\varprojlim_{s}\varinjlim_{r}\Pi_{r,s}^{\bullet}
, in the limit we get a
K_{p}
-equivariant homotopy equivalence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi^{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
.

By
[
Eme06b
, Proposition 1.2.12]
, we have
\mathrm{H}^{\ast}(A^{\bullet})\simeq\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}^{\ast}(K^{p},{\mathcal{O}}_{L})
computes completed cohomology. It is also evident by the above torsor description that as
K_{p}
-representation, we have continuous isomorphisms
\Pi^{n}\simeq\mathcal{C}(K_{p},\mathcal{O}_{L})^{\oplus m_{n}}
for some
m_{n}\geq 0
, and so
\Pi^{\bullet}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{L}}L
is in fact a complex of
injective
admissible Banach representations of
K_{p}
. Thus, in the derived category of admissible Banach
K_{p}
-representations,
\Pi^{\bullet}
computes completed cohomology. Likewise, by
[
Fu22b
, Proposition 5.3]
,
A\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{L}}L
is a Banach space with a continuous action of
G(F_{p})
.

We can also make these same definitions for compactly supported cohomology and boundary cohomology and get complexes and
K_{p}
-equivariant homotopy equivalences
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{c}^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{c}^{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
,
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\partial}^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{\partial}^{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
. Then there is a short exact sequence
0\to\Pi_{c}^{\bullet}\to\Pi^{\bullet}\to\Pi_{\partial}^{\bullet}\to 0
of complexes of injective admissible
K_{p}
-representations and a short exact sequence of complexes
0\to A_{c}^{\bullet}\to A^{\bullet}\to A_{\partial}\to 0
which are continuous maps of Banach spaces, which are compatible with the quasi-isomorphisms
\Pi_{c}^{\bullet}\to A_{c}^{\bullet},\Pi^{\bullet}\to A^{\bullet},\Pi_{\partial}^{\bullet}\to A^{\bullet}
. Again, we stress that there is no obvious reason the homotopy inverses
i
should be compatible on the nose with these sequences. In the sequel, we will typically write
\Pi=\Pi(K^{p})
for the complexes constructed in this subsection.

2.3
Review of
G
-extensions and derived Jacquet functors

We review the theory of
G
-extensions that Fu uses to define his derived Jacquet functors of completed cohomology.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
\mathbf{H}/{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
be an algebraic group. Let
H=\mathbf{H}(F_{p})
and
H_{0}\subset H
a compact open subgroup. An
H
-extension
is the data
(C,C^{\prime},i,p)
where
C
is a bounded complex of admissible Banach representation of
H_{0}
,
C^{\prime}
is a bounded complex of Banach representations of
H
, and
i,p
are
H_{0}
-equivariant, continuous homotopy equivalences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
.

Remark 2.5
.

Later on in
Section

3
it will be convenient for us to call certain homotopy equivalences between complexes “
B
-extensions” even though they do not take the form above. In these cases, we will simply be referring to a
B_{0}
-equivariant homotopy equivalence to a complex with an action of
B\supset B_{0}
, where
B_{0}
is a compact open subgroup. However, in our cases these
B
-extensions will be induced by an
H
-extensions of the form mentioned above.

The motivation for this of course is that
(\Pi(K^{p})^{\bullet},A^{\bullet},i,p)
with a choice of homotopy equivalences form a
G(F_{p})
-extension in the sense of
Definition

2.4
.

Suppose now that our algebraic group
G/F
is in fact a connected reductive group such that
G
is split over
F_{\nu}
for all
\nu\mid p
. Let
({\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{N}})
be a Borel and maximal torus of
G
with Levi decomposition
{\mathbf{B}}={\mathbf{T}}{\mathbf{N}}
for
{\mathbf{T}}
a maximal torus. Write
G_{p}=G(F_{p}),B={\mathbf{B}}(F_{p}),N={\mathbf{N}}(F_{p}),T={\mathbf{T}}(F_{p})
. Fix
K_{p}
a compact open subgroup of
G_{p}
and for any subgroup
H\subset G_{p}
set
H_{0}:=H\cap K_{p}
. Then set
T^{+}:=\{t\in T:tN_{0}t^{-1}\subseteq N_{0}\}
.

Let
C
be a complex of injective admissible Banach
K_{p}
-representations, equipped with a
G_{p}
-extension. We are interested in its locally analytic vectors
C^{\operatorname{la},i}:=(C^{i})^{K_{p}-\operatorname{la}}
. The functor of locally analytic vectors is exact on admissible representations by a well-known result of Schneider–Teitelbaum
[
ST03
, Theorem 7.1]
. Likewise,
[
Fu22b
, Lemma 3.8, Corollary 3.9]
show that from a derived category perspective it is reasonable to consider the complex of
N_{0}
-invariants
C^{\operatorname{la},N_{0}}
.
Now let
z\in T^{+}
be an element such that
\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}z^{n}N_{0}z^{-n}=1
. Using the
G_{p}
-extension, we may define an action of
z
on
C^{\bullet}
via the formula
\widetilde{z}\cdot v:=(p\circ z\circ i)v
. This action commutes with the action of other elements of
T^{+}
given by a similar formula, up to homotopy.
On
C^{\operatorname{la},N_{0}}
we may then define an action of
z
via the formula

U_{z}v:=\sum_{n\in N_{0}/zN_{0}z^{-1}}n\cdot\widetilde{z}\cdot v.

Remark 2.6
.

We note that throughout this paper, our actions of
U_{z}
differ from Emerton’s normalisation in
[
Eme06a
]
, where he multiplies by an averaging factor
\delta_{B}(z)=[N_{0}:zN_{0}z^{-1}]^{-1}
. Thus, our action of
U_{z}
is Emerton’s
U_{z}
action twisted by the modulus character
\delta_{B}^{-1}
. We use this normalisation to make some aspects of
Section

4
less cumbersome to write down.

Now recall that if
H
is a (topologically finitely generated) abelian locally
{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
-analytic group, we say a representation
V\in\operatorname{Rep}_{\rm{la.c}}(H)
is
essentially admissible
if its strong dual
V^{\prime}
is coadmissible over the Fréchet–Stein algebra
C^{\operatorname{an}}(\widehat{H},L)\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{\widehat{H}}(\widehat{H})
. Equivalently by
[
Eme06b
, Proposition 2.3.2]

V^{\prime}
is a global sections of a coherent sheaf on
\widehat{H}
.
Letting
Y^{+}\subset T
be the closed submonoid generated by
T_{0},z
and
Y
the closed subgroup generated by
T_{0},z,z^{-1}
, the above formula defines an action of
Y^{+}
on
C^{\operatorname{la},N_{0}}
. We can then take the
finite slope part
(as in
[
Eme06a
, Section 3]
) using the formula

(C^{i,\operatorname{la},N_{0}})_{\rm{fs}}=\mathcal{L}_{b,Y^{+}}(\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{an}}(\widehat{Y},L),C^{i,\operatorname{la},N_{0}}).

We define the
derived Jacquet functors
via the formula
(J_{B}(C^{\operatorname{la}}))^{i}:=(C^{\bullet,\operatorname{la},N_{0}})_{\rm{fs}}
.

One main result of
[
Fu22b
]
is that with respect to this natural definition, we have
J_{B}(C^{\operatorname{la}})
is a complex of essentially admissible representations of
Y
with
Y
-equivariant differentials (which are thus automatically strict). The spaces we are principally interested in are the cohomology groups
\mathrm{H}^{\ast}(J_{B}(C^{\operatorname{la}}))
, which are in fact essentially admissible
T
-representations by
[
Fu22b
, Lemma 3.19]
.
[
Fu22b
, Proposition 4.9]
shows that we may compute
\mathrm{H}^{i}(J_{B}(C^{\operatorname{la}}))\simeq(\mathrm{H}_{\operatorname{haus}}^{i}(C^{\operatorname{la},N_{0}}))_{\operatorname{fs}}
, where for a complex of topological vector spaces
(M^{\bullet},d^{\bullet})
,
\mathrm{H}_{\operatorname{haus}}^{i}(M^{\bullet}):=\ker(d^{i})/\overline{\operatorname{im}(d^{i-1})}
, denotes the
Hausdorff
cohomology (that is, we quotient by the
closure
of the image of
d^{i-1}
). This property implies that
\mathrm{H}^{i}(J_{B}(C^{\operatorname{la}}))
is independent of the choice of
i
(with
p
fixed) or of the choice of
p
(with
i
fixed) by
[
Fu22b
, Corollary 4.10]
. Since the derived category formalism here is a little lacking, it is not a priori obvious that this definition is independent of the choice of representing complex
C^{\bullet}
. We content ourselves with the following lemma:

Lemma 2.7
.

Suppose we have a
K_{p}
-equivariant homotopy equivalence
f:S_{1}\to S_{2}
,
g:S_{2}\to S_{1}
between complexes
S_{1},S_{2}
of injective admissible
K_{p}
-representations. Suppose also
S_{2}
is a equipped with a
G
-extension. Then we have a topological isomorphism of essentially admissible representations of
T

\mathrm{H}^{i}(J_{B}(S_{1}^{\operatorname{la}}))\simeq\mathrm{H}^{i}(J_{B}(S_{2}^{\operatorname{la}})),

where
J_{B}(S_{1}^{\operatorname{la}})
is defined by composing the
G
-extension of
S_{2}
with
f,g
.

Proof.

Let
(A,i,p)
denote the
G
-extension on
S_{2}
. The homotopy equivalence between
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
induces one between
S_{1}^{\operatorname{la},N_{0}}
and
S_{2}^{\operatorname{la},N_{0}}
. In particular the natural induced maps on cohomology
\mathrm{H}^{\bullet}(S_{1}^{\operatorname{la},N_{0}})\to\mathrm{H}^{\bullet}(S_{2}^{\operatorname{la},N_{0}})
are continuous isomorphisms, which moreover intertwines
g\circ p\circ z\circ i\circ f
and
p\circ z\circ i
, since
g\circ f=f\circ g=\operatorname{id}
on cohomology. Thus, we may pass to Hausdorff quotients and finite slope parts.
∎

2.8
Jacquet functors in terms of homology

It is sometimes more convenient for us to work with Jacquet functors in terms of homology. While the above use of
G_{p}
-extensions did not make explicit reference to locally symmetric spaces, the homology approach will. Continue to assume
G/F
is split at all places
\nu\mid p
. Then let
I:=\overline{N}^{1}\cdot T_{0}\cdot N_{0}
be the Iwahori subgroup, i.e. the preimage of
B({\mathcal{O}}_{F}\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}}{\mathbb{F}}_{p})
under the reduction map
G({\mathcal{O}}_{F,p})\to G({\mathcal{O}}_{F}\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}}{\mathbb{F}}_{p})
. Likewise, for any
n\geq 1
let
\overline{N}^{n}=\overline{N}^{1}\cap I^{n}\subset\overline{N}^{1}
be the intersection of
\overline{N}^{1}
with the kernel of the reduction mod
p^{n}
map
G({\mathcal{O}}_{F,p})\to G({\mathcal{O}}_{F}\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}}{\mathbf{Z}}/p^{n})
.

Now suppose that the tame level
K^{p}
is sufficiently small, so that
K^{p}I
is neat (and thus good). After fixing a finite triangulation of the Borel–Serre compactification
\overline{X}_{K^{p}I}^{G}
, we have a complex
C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p})
and a
{\mathbf{Z}}[I]
-linear homotopy equivalence to
C(K^{p})
, which induces a
D(I)
-linear homotopy equivalence

\hbox to375.7pt{\vbox to20.86pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 187.84604pt\lower-11.21997pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-88.17798pt}{-7.70612pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -122.01 -10.66)} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{

Choosing
z\in T^{++}
, we get an induced action of an operator
\widetilde{z}:=p\circ z\circ i
on
C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I))
. Letting
C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I))_{N_{0}}
denote the Hausdorff coinvariants under the right
N_{0}
-action, we have an isomorphism
C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I))_{N_{0}}\simeq C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I)_{N_{0}})
, since for all
i
we have
C_{i}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I))\simeq D(I)^{\oplus m_{i}}
for some
m_{i}\geq 0
. We can then define an Atkin-Lehner-like operator via the formula
c\cdot U_{1}z:=\sum_{n^{\prime}\in N_{0}/zN_{0}z^{-1}}cn^{\prime}\widetilde{z}
. With respect to this action, we may pass to the (dual)
finite slope part

(C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I)_{N_{0}}))_{\operatorname{fs}}
and define the dual Jacquet functor

J_{B}^{\vee}(C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I))):=C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I)_{N_{0}})\widehat{\otimes}_{L[z]}L\{\{z^{\pm 1}\}\}.

We can make analogous definitions for complexes involving
C^{\partial,\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p})
or
C^{\operatorname{BM},\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p})
.

Again, the proof of
[
Fu22b
, Proposition 3.20]
implies that
J_{B}^{\vee}(C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I)))
is a complex of coadmissible
\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{Y}}(\widehat{Y})
-modules.
In fact, this proof (in particular, the arguments contained in
[
Eme06a
, Lemma 4.2.11, Lemma 4.2.19]
) only needs the following setup: let
V_{\bullet}
be a bounded complex of finite free
D(I)
-modules, and choose
z\in T^{++}
such that
z^{-1}\overline{N}^{n}z\subset\overline{N}^{n+1}
. Suppose moreover
V_{\bullet}
is equipped with a
T_{0}
-linear operator
\widetilde{z}
such that for all
n\in N_{0}
we have
\widetilde{z}n=znz^{-1}\widetilde{z}
and for all
\overline{n}\in\overline{N}^{1}
, we have
\overline{n}\widetilde{z}=\widetilde{z}\cdot(z^{-1}\overline{n}z)
. Then the same proof as in
[
Eme06a
, Proposition 4.2.28]
gives that with respect to the same formula for
U_{1}z
, the complex
(V_{\bullet,N_{0}})\otimes_{L[z]}L\{\{z^{\pm 1}\}\}
is a complex of coadmissible
\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{Y}}(\widehat{Y})
-modules.

This first lemma then shows that we can also take the finite slope part after taking homology.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
V_{\bullet}
be a bounded complex of finite free
D(I)
-modules, and choose
z\in T^{++}
such that
z\overline{N}^{n}z^{-1}\subset\overline{N}^{n+1}
. Suppose moreover
V_{\bullet}
is equipped with a
T_{0}
-linear operator
\widetilde{z}
such that for all
n\in N_{0}
we have
\widetilde{z}n=znz^{-1}\widetilde{z}
and for all
\overline{n}\in\overline{N}^{1}
, we have
\overline{n}\widetilde{z}=\widetilde{z}\cdot(z\overline{n}z^{-1})
. Then we have an isomorphism of coadmissible
\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{Y}}(\widehat{Y})
-modules
\mathrm{H}_{i}((V_{\bullet,N_{0}})_{\operatorname{fs}})\simeq\mathrm{H}_{i,\operatorname{haus}}((V_{\bullet,N_{0}}))_{\operatorname{fs}}
.

Proof.

Setting
d_{n}:V_{n}\to V_{n-1}
for any
n
, we just need to show that
\ker(d_{n})\widehat{\otimes}_{L[z]}L\{\{z,z^{-1}\}\}=\ker(d_{n}\widehat{\otimes}_{L[z]}L\{\{z,z^{-1}\}\}
and
\overline{\operatorname{im}(d_{n})}\widehat{\otimes}_{L[z]}L\{\{z,z^{-1}\}\}=\operatorname{im}(d_{n}\widehat{\otimes}_{L[z]}L\{\{z,z^{-1}\}\}
.
For the first isomorphism, we wish to show the map

\ker(d_{n})\widehat{\otimes}_{L[z]}L\{\{z,z^{-1}\}\}\to V_{n}\widehat{\otimes}_{L[z]}L\{\{z,z^{-1}\}\}

is injective. Note that this map is strict since both modules are coadmissible over
\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{Y}}
. The map
\ker(d_{n})\to V_{n}
is injective and
L\{\{z,z^{-1}\}\}
is flat over
L[z]
, so the induced map before completing remains injective. Then
[
Fu22b
, Proposition 4.8]
gives the desired injectivity.

The second isomorphism follows a similar strategy: the map
d_{n}
factors through
\overline{\operatorname{im}(d_{n})}
, and it suffices to show
V_{n}\widehat{\otimes}_{L[z]}L\{\{z,z^{-1}\}\}\to\overline{\operatorname{im}(d_{n})}\widehat{\otimes}_{L[z]}L\{\{z,z^{-1}\}\}
is surjective. Note this map is automatically strict since both are coadmissible over
\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{Y}}(\widehat{Y})
. Now by the same duality argument (but going from Frechet spaces to compact type spaces instead of the other way around) as in
[
Fu22b
, Proposition 4.9]
, it suffices to show
(\overline{\operatorname{im}(d_{n})}^{\prime})_{\operatorname{fs}}\to((V_{n})^{\prime})_{\operatorname{fs}}
is injective.
\overline{\operatorname{im}(d_{n})}^{\prime}\to(V_{n})^{\prime}
is already injective by Hahn–Banach, and so the desired isomorphism amounts to the left-exactness of the finite slope functor from
[
Eme06a
, Proposition 3.2.6 (iii)]
.
∎

We can use this lemma to justify working on the homology side to study the derived Jacquet functors of
[
Fu22b
]
.

Corollary 2.10
.

Let
\Pi(K^{p})
be the complex of injective admissible
I
-representations computing
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}^{\ast}(K^{p},L)
as defined in
Section

2.2.2
, equipped with a
G_{p}
-extension. Then the strong dual
\mathrm{H}^{\ast}(J_{B}(\Pi(K^{p})^{\operatorname{la}}))^{\prime}
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{H}_{\ast}(J_{B}^{\vee}(C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p},D(I))))
. Analogous isomorphisms hold for boundary and compactly supported cohomology.

Proof.

By
Lemma

2.9
, the group
\mathrm{H}_{\ast}(J_{B}^{\vee}(C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p},D(I))))
is equal to
\mathrm{H}_{\ast,\operatorname{haus}}(C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I)_{N_{0}}))\widehat{\otimes}_{L[Y^{+}]}\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{Y}}
. Note that the complexes
C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I))
and
(\Pi(K^{p})^{\operatorname{la}})^{\prime}
are quasi-isomorphic perfect complexes of
D(I)
-modules, and so appealing again to
[
Wei94
, 10.4.7]
implies these complexes are
D(I)
-equivariantly homotopic. Thus, there is a homotopy equivalence between
((\Pi(K^{p})^{\operatorname{la}})^{\prime})_{N_{0}}\simeq(\Pi(K^{p})^{\operatorname{la},N_{0}})^{\prime}
and
C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I))_{N_{0}}\simeq C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I)_{N_{0}})
(we can pass to Hausdorff convariants since the homotopy equivalences in both directions are automatically continuous). Moreover, the action of
\widetilde{z}
on each complexes are homotopic, as both are induced from a choice of homotopy inverse to the quasi-isomorphism
C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p})\to C(K^{p})
. Thus, we in fact have a topological isomorphism
\mathrm{H}_{\ast}((\Pi(K^{p})^{N_{0}})^{\prime})\simeq\mathrm{H}_{\ast}(C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I)_{N_{0}}))
equivariant for the
Y^{+}
action on both sides, and thus an isomorphism
\mathrm{H}_{\ast,\operatorname{haus}}((\Pi(K^{p})^{\operatorname{la},N_{0}})^{\prime})_{\operatorname{fs}}\simeq\mathrm{H}_{\ast,\operatorname{haus}}(C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I)_{N_{0}}))_{\operatorname{fs}}\simeq\mathrm{H}_{\ast}(J_{B}^{\vee}(C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I))))
.

The last observation needed is that for any
z\in T^{++}
satisfying
z^{-1}N^{\operatorname{1}}z\subset N^{\operatorname{2}}
and any lift of
\widetilde{z}
of the
z
-action to the complex
(\Pi(K^{p})^{\operatorname{la},N_{0}})^{\prime}
satisfying the conditions in
Lemma

2.9
, then taking the finite slope part with respect to
\widetilde{z}
yields
\mathrm{H}_{\ast,\operatorname{haus}}((\Pi(K^{p})^{\operatorname{la},N_{0}})^{\prime})_{\operatorname{fs}}\simeq\mathrm{H}_{\ast}(J_{B}(\Pi(K^{p})^{\operatorname{la}})^{{}^{\prime}})
. Again, the statements for boundary and compactly supported (co)homology are analogous.
∎

For proving
Theorem

1.5
, it will be convenient to have a variant of the Atkin–Lehner type action
U_{z}
, which we will denote
U_{2}z
(as opposed to
U_{1}z
defined above). We will show
U_{1}z,U_{2}z
are homotopic to each other (and thus yield isomorphic finite slope parts on cohomology by
Lemma

2.9
).

U_{2}z
comes from an action of
z
on both
C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p})
and on certain coefficient modules. First, note there is a natural right
T^{+}
-action on the space
I/N_{0}\simeq\overline{N}^{1}T_{0}
given by
(\overline{n}tN_{0})\ast z:=z^{-1}\overline{n}ztN_{0}
. This action induces a left
T^{+}
-action on
\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{la}}(I,L)^{N_{0}}\simeq\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{la}}(I/N_{0},L)
given by
(z\cdot f)(g)=f(g\ast z)
where
g\ast\delta
is the action defined above. In particular, we may view this action as arising as a composition

\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{la}}(\overline{N}^{1}T_{0},L)\xrightarrow{\operatorname{res}}\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{la}}(z^{-1}\overline{N}^{1}T_{0}z,L)\xrightarrow{\operatorname{conj}_{z^{-1}}}\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{la}}(\overline{N}^{1}T_{0},L).

Passing to the topological dual
(\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{la}}(I,L)^{N_{0}})^{\prime}\simeq D(I)_{N_{0}}
then gives a right
T^{+}
-action on
D(I)_{N_{0}}
which occurs as a factorization

D(I)_{N_{0}}\simeq D(\overline{N}^{1}T_{0})\xrightarrow{\operatorname{conj}_{z^{-1}}}D(z^{-1}\overline{N}^{1}zT_{0})\hookrightarrow D(\overline{N}^{1}T_{0})\simeq D(I)_{N_{0}}
. Note that on distribution algebras, this action amounts to the natural left and right multiplication
v\mapsto z^{-1}vz
. Denote this action by
\cdot_{c}z
.
Fixing
{\mathbf{Z}}[I]
-linear homotopy equivalences
i,p
between
C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p})
and
C(K^{p})
, we first define an action of
z
on
C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p})
by the formula
\widetilde{z}:=p\circ z\circ i
.

We then define the operator
U_{2}z
on
C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I)_{N_{0}})
as follows: Note that the double coset
IzI\simeq I/(zIz^{-1}\cap I)\simeq N/zN_{0}z^{-1}
, so we can write
IzI=\bigsqcup_{n^{\prime}\in N_{0}/zN_{0}z^{-1}}n^{\prime}zI
. Then we define

(\sigma\otimes m)\cdot U_{2}z\mapsto\sum_{n^{\prime}\in N_{0}/zN_{0}z^{-1}}(\sigma\otimes m)n^{\prime}z=\sum_{n^{\prime}}\sigma n^{\prime}\widetilde{z}\otimes((n^{\prime})^{-1}m)\cdot_{c}z,

(3)

Lemma 2.11
.

Fixing
z\in T^{++}
, the two Hecke actions
U_{1}z
and
U_{2}z
on
C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{P}I,D(I)_{N_{0}})
are
D(T_{0})
-equivariantly homotopic.

Proof.

Recall that the action
U_{1}z
is induced by a homotopy equivalence from the right action of
z
on
C(K^{p})\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}[G]}D(G)
, which we denote
\widetilde{z}
. The formula for the action can then be written as

C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I)_{N_{0}})\xrightarrow{\sum_{n^{\prime}\in N_{0}/zN_{0}z^{-1}}n^{\prime}}C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I)_{zN_{0}z^{-1}})\xrightarrow{\cdot\widetilde{z}}C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I)_{N_{0}}).

On the other hand, up to homotopy the Hecke action
U_{2}z
is given by the composition

C(K^{p})\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}[I]}D(I)_{N_{0}}\xrightarrow{\sum_{n^{\prime}\in N_{0}/zN_{0}z^{-1}}(-)n^{\prime}\otimes(n^{\prime})^{-1}(-)}C(K^{p})\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}[zIz^{-1}]}D(zIz^{-1})_{zN_{0}z^{-1}}\xrightarrow{r_{z}}C(K^{p})\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}[I]}D(I)_{N_{0}},

where
r_{z}(c\otimes v)=cz\otimes v\cdot_{c}z
.
The key fact then is that natural right action of
g
on
C(K^{p})\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}[G]}D(G)

is chain homotopic to the action
r_{g}(c\otimes v)=cg\otimes g^{-1}vg
by the discussion in
[
JNWE24
, p. 59]
.
Note that for
m\in D(I)_{N_{0}}
we also have
(n^{\prime})^{-1}m(n^{\prime})=(n^{\prime})^{-1}m
for
n^{\prime}\in N_{0}
, and thus
\sigma n^{\prime}\otimes(n^{\prime})^{-1}m=r_{n^{\prime}}(\sigma\otimes m)
. In particular, the two formulae above are chain homotopic by the cited fact.
∎

2.12
Setting of CM fields

We now recall the groups and locally symmetric spaces of specific interest. Let
F\supset F^{+}
be an imaginary CM field and its totally real subfield. Let
c\in\operatorname{Aut}(F/F^{+})
denote complex conjugation. Let
\Psi_{n}
be the
n\times n
matrix with
1
’s on the anti-diagonal and
0
’s elsewhere. Then we let
\widetilde{G}/{\mathcal{O}}_{F^{+}}
be the group scheme with functor of points

\widetilde{G}(R):=\{g\in\operatorname{GL}_{2n}(R\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{F^{+}}}{\mathcal{O}}_{F}):{}^{t}g\begin{pmatrix}0&\psi_{n}\\
-\psi_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}g^{c}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\psi_{n}\\
-\psi_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}\}

The generic fibre
\widetilde{G}_{F^{+}}
is a quasi-split unitary group
U(n,n)/F^{+}
of signature
(n,n)
, which after the quadratic base-change
F/F^{+}
becomes isomorphic to
\operatorname{GL}_{2n}/F
. In particular, if
\overline{\nu}\in S_{p}(F^{+})
is a place which splits in
F
, then any choice of
\nu|\overline{\nu}
in
F
determines an isomorphism

\iota_{\nu}:\widetilde{G}(F_{\overline{\nu}}^{+})\simeq\operatorname{GL}_{2n}(F_{\nu}),

(4)

induced by the isomorphism
F_{\overline{\nu}}^{+}\otimes_{F^{+}}F\simeq F_{\nu}\times F_{\nu^{c}}
and composing the resulting embedding
\widetilde{G}(F_{\overline{\nu}})\subset\operatorname{GL}_{2n}(F_{\nu})\times\operatorname{GL}_{2n}(F_{\nu^{c}})
with projection onto the first group.

We let
T\subset B\subset\widetilde{G}
be subgroups of diagonal and upper triangular matrices in
\widetilde{G}
, respectively. We also let
G\subset P\subset\widetilde{G}
be the Siegel parabolic consisting of block diagonal and block upper-triangular matrices with blocks of size
n\times n
, respectively. Then there is a Levi decomposition
P=U\rtimes G
, where
U
is the unipotent radical of
P
of block unipotent matrices. Likewise, we can identify
G=\operatorname{Res}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{F}/{\mathcal{O}}_{F^{+}}}\operatorname{GL}_{n}
via the map
\begin{pmatrix}A&\\
&D\end{pmatrix}\mapsto D\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(R\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{F^{+}}}{\mathcal{O}}_{F})
. We will also (for convenience mainly) use
\overline{P}\subset\widetilde{G}
the opposite parabolic of block lower triangular matrices, which has a Levi decomposition
\overline{P}=\overline{U}\rtimes G
where
\overline{U}
is the unipotent radical of block lower unipotent matrices. We similarly let
T_{n}\subset B_{n}\subset G=\operatorname{Res}_{\mathcal{O}_{F}/\mathcal{O}_{F^{+}}}\operatorname{GL}_{n}
be the standard diagonal maximal torus and upper triangular Borel subgroup.
Note also under the identification
\iota_{\nu}
in (
4
),
G(F_{\overline{\nu}}^{+})\simeq\operatorname{GL}_{n}(F_{\nu})\times\operatorname{GL}_{n}(F_{\nu})
are the split block diagonal matrices, and
T(F_{\overline{\nu}}^{+})\simeq T_{n}(F_{\nu})\times T_{n}(F_{\nu})
.

More generally, suppose that
p
is a prime such that each place
\overline{\nu}\in S_{p}(F^{+})
splits in
F
. Then choose
\widetilde{S}_{p}\subset S_{p}(F)
a subset of places such that
S_{p}(F)=\widetilde{S}_{p}\sqcup\widetilde{S}_{p}^{c}
.
Using (
4
), such a choice determines an isomorphism

\widetilde{G}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{F^{+}}}{\mathcal{O}}_{F^{+},p}\simeq\prod_{\widetilde{\nu}\in\widetilde{S}_{p}}\operatorname{GL}_{2n,{\mathcal{O}}_{F_{\widetilde{\nu}}}}

with again the parabolic
P\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{F^{+}}}{\mathcal{O}}_{F^{+},p}
identifying with the block upper triangular matrices in
\operatorname{GL}_{2n}
, and likewise
T_{\mathcal{O}_{F^{+},p}}\simeq(T_{n})_{\mathcal{O}_{F,p}}
. We will implicitly use this identification throughout the rest of the paper. To simplify notation, for good compact open subgroups
\widetilde{K}\subset\widetilde{G}({\mathbb{A}}_{F^{+}}^{\infty}),K\subset\operatorname{GL}_{n}({\mathbb{A}}_{F}^{\infty})
we also let
\widetilde{X}_{\widetilde{K}}:=X_{\widetilde{K}}^{\widetilde{G}}
and
X_{K}:=X_{K}^{G}
be the symmetric spaces associated to
\widetilde{G}
and
G
. We note the dimensions are
\dim_{{\mathbf{R}}}\widetilde{X}_{\widetilde{K}}=2d=2n^{2}[F^{+}:{\mathbf{Q}}]
and
\dim_{{\mathbf{R}}}X_{K}=d-1=n^{2}[F^{+}:{\mathbf{Q}}]-1
.

2.12.1
Hecke algebras

We now discuss Hecke algebras. For our work we need to localize at maximal ideals of Hecke algebras, sometimes on the level of complexes. We recall how to make sense of such notions with derived Jacquet functors.

For any algebraic group
H
over a number field
E
and
S\supset S_{p}(F)
a finite set of finite places of
E
let
H^{S}:=H({\mathbb{A}}_{E}^{S\cup\infty})
and
K_{H}^{S}\subset H^{S}
a compact open subgroup.
Then let
\mathcal{H}(H^{S},K_{H}^{S})
be the abstract Hecke algebra of compactly supported,
K_{H}^{S}
-biinvariant,
{\mathbf{Z}}
-valued functions on
H^{S}
. Setting
K^{S}=\prod_{v\notin S\cup\infty}\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F_{\nu}})
, the spherical Hecke algebra for
G=\operatorname{GL}_{n}/F
is defined to be
{\mathbf{T}}^{S}:=\mathcal{H}(\operatorname{GL}_{n}({\mathbb{A}}_{F}^{S\cup\infty}),K^{S})\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}}{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}
, which is naturally a commutative
{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}
-algebra. We can make similar definitions for
\widetilde{G}
. Suppose
S
satisfies
S=S^{c}
, and let
\overline{S}
be the set of places of
F^{+}
lying below
S
. Then define
\widetilde{K}^{S}:=\widetilde{K}^{\overline{S}}=\prod_{\overline{\nu}\notin\overline{S}\cup\infty}\widetilde{G}(\mathcal{O}_{F_{\overline{\nu}}^{+}})
, and
\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}^{S}:=\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{G}({\mathbb{A}}_{F^{+}}^{\overline{S}\cup\infty}),\widetilde{K}^{S})\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}}{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}
.

Any Hecke algebra (such as
{\mathbf{T}}^{S}
or
\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}^{S}
) is spanned by indicator functions on double cosets
[K^{S}gK^{S}]
for
g\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}({\mathbb{A}}_{F}^{S\cup\infty})
. Then for any left
K_{p}
-module
M
we can define an action of
{\mathbf{T}}^{S}
on
C(K^{p})\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}[K_{p}]}M
via double cosets
K^{S}gK^{S}=\sqcup_{j}h_{j}K^{S}
and setting

(\sigma\otimes m)[K^{S}gK^{S}]:=\sum_{j}\sigma h_{j}\otimes m.

Up to homotopy, this formula also defines the action of any double coset
[K^{S}gK^{S}]
on
C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}K_{p},M)
.
Note that since we are looking at Hecke operators away from
p
, these operators make no reference to the coefficients
M
, and thus when
M=D(I),D(I)_{N_{0}}
, the action defined is dual (up to homotopy) to the Hecke action on the complex
A^{\bullet}
computing completed cohomology.

We use “big Hecke algebras,” as defined in
[
GN22
, 2.1.10]
.
For
U_{p}\subset K_{p}
an open normal subgroup, we define

{\mathbf{T}}_{G}^{S}(K^{p}U_{p},{\mathcal{O}}_{L}/p^{m}):=\operatorname{im}({\mathbf{T}}^{S}\to\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}(\mathcal{O}_{L}/p^{m}[K_{p}/U_{p}])}(C(K^{p}U_{p},{\mathcal{O}}_{L}/p^{m}))).

Then we define the
big Hecke algebra
to be

{\mathbf{T}}_{G}^{S}(K^{p}):=\varprojlim_{U_{p},m}{\mathbf{T}}_{G}^{S}(K^{p}U_{p},{\mathcal{O}}_{L}/p^{m}),

where the transition maps are induced by the functorial maps
\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}(\mathcal{O}_{L}/p^{m^{\prime}}[K_{p}/U_{p}^{\prime}])}(C(K^{p}U_{p},{\mathcal{O}}_{L}/p^{m}))\to\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}(\mathcal{O}_{L}/p^{m}[K_{p}/U_{p}])}(C(K^{p}U_{p}^{\prime},{\mathcal{O}}_{L}/p^{m^{\prime}}))
where
U_{p}^{\prime}\subset U_{p}\subset K_{p}
and
m^{\prime}\geq m
.
{\mathbf{T}}_{G}^{S}(K^{p})
comes equipped with an inverse limit topology. A foundational fact
[
GN22
, Lemma 2.1.14]
then is that
{\mathbf{T}}_{G}^{S}(K^{p})
is a semi-local
{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}
-algebra, and if
\mathfrak{m}_{1},\dots\mathfrak{m}_{r}
denotes its finite set of maximal ideals then there is a decomposition

{\mathbf{T}}_{G}^{S}(K^{p})={\mathbf{T}}_{G}^{S}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}_{1}}\times\dots\times{\mathbf{T}}_{G}^{S}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}_{r}},

(5)

where each localisation
{\mathbf{T}}_{G}^{S}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}_{i}}
is
\mathfrak{m}_{i}
-adically complete and separated. Moreover, for any
s
and
U_{p}
pro-
p
, the maximal ideals of
{\mathbf{T}}_{G}^{S}(K^{p}U_{p},{\mathcal{O}}_{L}/p^{s})
are in bijection with those of
{\mathbf{T}}_{G}^{S}(K^{p})
, and likewise we have
{\mathbf{T}}_{G}^{S}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}\simeq\varprojlim_{U_{p},m}{\mathbf{T}}_{G}^{S}(K^{p}U_{p},{\mathcal{O}}_{L}/p^{m})_{\mathfrak{m}}
.

Following
[
Fu22b
, §6]
, for any maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}\subset{\mathbf{T}}_{G}^{S}(K^{p})
we can define localised complexes
C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p},\mathcal{O}_{L}[[K_{p}]])_{\mathfrak{m}}:=R\lim_{r}\lim_{n}C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p},\mathcal{O}_{L}/p^{r}[K_{p}/K_{p}^{n}])_{\mathfrak{m}}
, which via (
5
) admits a direct sum decomposition in the derived category
\mathsf{D}({\mathcal{O}}_{L}[[K_{p}]])

C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p},\mathcal{O}_{L}[[K_{p}]])\simeq\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{m}\in\operatorname{MaxSpec}({\mathbf{T}}_{G}^{S}(K^{p}))}C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p},\mathcal{O}_{L}[[K_{p}]])_{\mathfrak{m}}.

We can then
choose
a perfect complex of
{\mathcal{O}}_{L}[[K_{p}]]
-modules representing
C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p},\mathcal{O}_{L}[[K_{p}]])_{\mathfrak{m}}
, which by abuse of notation will also denote by the same symbol. These complexes also carry a natural homotopy equivalence to the complex
C(K^{p},{\mathcal{O}}_{L}[[K_{p}]])_{\mathfrak{m}}
, which possesses an action of the full group
G(F_{p})
and of the Hecke algebra
{\mathbf{T}}_{G}^{S}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}
.
Upon tensoring with
D(K_{p})
and taking
N_{0}
-coinvariants, we likewise get complexes

C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p},D(K_{p})_{N_{0}})_{\mathfrak{m}}
, whose homology carries a natural action of
T_{G}^{S}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}
.
Likewise on the dual side, denote the dual of
C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p},L[[K_{p}]])_{\mathfrak{m}}
by
\Pi(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}
. Then we have homotopies
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi(K^{p})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
which on homology are the maps induced by the direct summand
{\mathbf{T}}_{G}^{S}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}\to{\mathbf{T}}_{G}^{S}(K^{p})
. Upon further composing, we then get a map
\Pi(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}\to A^{\bullet}\to A_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\bullet}
(as in
Section

2.2.2
) which is indeed a homotopy equivalence, prescribing
\Pi(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}
with the structure of
G
-extension.
Analogous constructions may be made for the unitary group
\widetilde{G}
, and for compactly supported and boundary cohomology.

We now recall the the Satake transfer
\mathcal{S}:\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}^{S}\to{\mathbf{T}}^{S}
.
Suppose
\widetilde{G}
is any reductive group over
{\mathcal{O}}_{F^{+}}
and
P=M\ltimes N
a parabolic subgroup. Given a good subgroup
K_{\widetilde{G}}\subset\widetilde{G}({\mathbb{A}}_{F^{+}}^{\infty})
, with
K_{M},K_{P},K_{N}
the corresponding intersections, we say that
K_{\widetilde{G}}
is
decomposed
with respect to
P=M\ltimes N
if
K_{P}=K_{M}\ltimes K_{N}
. If
K_{\widetilde{G}}
is decomposed with respect to
P
and
S
is a finite set of finite places such that
K_{\widetilde{G},\nu}
is hyperspecial for all
\nu\notin S
, we can define two maps on Hecke algebras, “restriction to
P
” and “integration along
N
”

r_{P}:\mathcal{H}({\widetilde{G}}^{S},K_{\widetilde{G}}^{S})\to\mathcal{H}(P^{S},K_{P}^{S}),\quad\quad r_{M}:\mathcal{H}(P^{S},K_{P}^{S})\to\mathcal{H}(M^{S},K_{M}^{S}),

and define the “unnormalised Satake transform” to be
\mathcal{S}:r_{M}\circ r_{P}:\mathcal{H}({\widetilde{G}}^{S},K_{\widetilde{G}}^{S})\to\mathcal{H}(M^{S},K_{M}^{S})
(see
[
NT16
, 2.2.3, 2.2.4]
).

We can also define the map
\mathcal{S}
in the case of big Hecke algebras. We specialize to the case of
\widetilde{G}/{\mathcal{O}}_{F^{+}}
the quasi-split unitary group,
P
the Siegel parabolic with Levi decomposition coming from
M=G=\operatorname{Res}_{F/F^{+}}\operatorname{GL}_{n}
. Suppose
\widetilde{U}_{p}\subset\widetilde{G}({\mathcal{O}}_{F^{+},p})
is such that
\widetilde{K}^{p}\widetilde{U}_{p}
is good and decomposed with respect to
P=GU
and
\mathfrak{m}\subset{\mathbf{T}}^{S}(K^{p}U_{p},{\mathcal{O}}_{L}/p^{m})
a non-Eisenstein maximal ideal (see
Section

2.32
). Then for all
m\geq 0
the map
\mathcal{S}
descends to a morphism

\mathcal{S}:{\mathbf{T}}_{\partial}^{S}(\widetilde{K}^{p}\widetilde{U}_{p},m)_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}\to{\mathbf{T}}_{G}^{S}(K^{p}U_{p},m)_{\mathfrak{m}},

by
[
ACC+23
, Theorem 2.4.4]
, where

{\mathbf{T}}_{\partial\widetilde{G}}^{S}(\widetilde{K}^{p}\widetilde{U}_{p},{\mathcal{O}}_{L}/p^{m})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}:=\operatorname{im}(\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}^{S}\to\operatorname{End}_{\mathsf{D}({\mathcal{O}}_{L}/p^{m}[\widetilde{K}_{p}/\widetilde{U}_{p}])}(C^{\partial}(\widetilde{K}^{p}\widetilde{U}_{p},{\mathcal{O}}_{L}/p^{m})))_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}.

Moreover, these morphisms are compatible with the transition maps induced by changing the level
\widetilde{U}_{p}
, since
\mathcal{S}
exists on the level of abstract Hecke algebras. Passing to the inverse limit, we get our induced map of big Hecke algebras

\mathcal{S}:{\mathbf{T}}_{\partial}^{S}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}\to{\mathbf{T}}_{G}^{S}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}.

In the sequel, we will typically suppress the
G,\widetilde{G}
from the notation and instead just consider the big Hecke algebras
\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}^{S}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}},{\mathbf{T}}^{S}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}
.

2.12.2
Weights for
G
and
\widetilde{G}

To get a sense of what our eigenvarieties will be interpolating, we recall what dominant algebraic weights look like for both
G
and
\widetilde{G}
. First fix
E
a finite extension which contains all the embeddings
F\hookrightarrow\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}
.

We start with
G
. We have that
X^{\ast}((\operatorname{Res}_{F/{\mathbf{Q}}}T_{n})_{E})\simeq({\mathbf{Z}}^{n})^{\operatorname{Hom}(F,E)}
via the identification
(\lambda_{\tau,i})_{\tau,i}\mapsto(\operatorname{diag}(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})\mapsto t_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\dots t_{n}^{\lambda_{n}})
. A character
(\lambda_{\tau,i})_{\tau,i}
is then
(\operatorname{Res}_{F/{\mathbf{Q}}}B)_{E}
-dominant if

\lambda_{\tau,1}\geq\dots\geq\lambda_{\tau,n}

for all
\tau\in\operatorname{Hom}(F,E)
. We let
{\mathbf{Z}}^{n}_{\operatorname{dom}}
denote the set of dominant tuples of
n
integers, so that
\lambda
is
(\operatorname{Res}_{F/{\mathbf{Q}}}B)_{E}
-dominant iff
\lambda\in({\mathbf{Z}}^{n}_{\operatorname{dom}})^{\operatorname{Hom}(F,E)}
. For any dominant weight
\lambda
we may define an irreducible algebraic representation
\mathsf{L}(\lambda)
of
G_{E}
of highest weight
\lambda
. If
\lambda\in({\mathbf{Z}}^{n})^{\operatorname{Hom}(F,E)}
, we can define its dual weight
\lambda^{\vee}
via
\lambda_{\tau,i}^{\vee}:=-\lambda_{\tau,n+1-i}
. Clearly if
\lambda
is dominant then so is
\lambda^{\vee}
, and in fact we have an isomorphism
\mathsf{L}(\lambda)^{\vee}\simeq L(\lambda^{\vee})
. We will also let
\mathsf{L}(\lambda)
denote the associated local system on
X_{K^{p}K_{p}}
and
\overline{X}_{K^{p}K_{p}}
.

Now for
\widetilde{G}
, suppose for simplicity (and it is the only case we will need) that every place
\overline{\nu}\in S_{p}(F^{+})
splits in
F
. For each
\overline{\nu}
, fix a
\nu\in S_{p}(F)
such that
\overline{\nu}|\nu
. These choices fix a lift
\tau:F\hookrightarrow E
for each
\overline{\tau}:F^{+}\hookrightarrow E
, and thus yields an isomorphism

(\operatorname{Res}_{F^{+}/{\mathbf{Q}}}\widetilde{G})_{E}\simeq\prod_{\tau\in\operatorname{Hom}(F^{+},E)}\operatorname{GL}_{2n,E},

and thus gives an identification of the character group
X^{\ast}((\operatorname{Res}_{F^{+}/{\mathbf{Q}}}T)_{E})\simeq({\mathbf{Z}}^{2n})^{\operatorname{Hom}(F^{+},E)}
. The
(\operatorname{Res}_{F^{+}/{\mathbf{Q}}}B)_{E}
-dominant weights are precisely those in
({\mathbf{Z}}^{2n}_{\operatorname{dom}})^{\operatorname{Hom}(F^{+},E)}
.
