We also define a composite isomorphism
({\mathbf{Z}}^{n})^{\operatorname{Hom}(F,E)}\simeq X^{\ast}((\operatorname{Res}_{F/{\mathbf{Q}}}T_{n})_{E})\simeq X^{\ast}((\operatorname{Res}_{F^{+}/{\mathbf{Q}}}T)_{E})\simeq({\mathbf{Z}}^{2n})^{\operatorname{Hom}(F^{+},E)}

mapping
\lambda\in({\mathbf{Z}}^{n})^{\operatorname{Hom}(F,E)}
to
\widetilde{\lambda}\in({\mathbf{Z}}^{2n})^{\operatorname{Hom}(F^{+},E)}
via

\widetilde{\lambda}_{\tau}=(-w_{0}^{\operatorname{GL}_{n}}\lambda_{\tau\circ c},\lambda_{\tau})=(-\lambda_{\tau c,n},\dots,-\lambda_{\tau c,1},\lambda_{\tau,1},\dots,\lambda_{\tau,n}).

We can similarly associate to a dominant
\widetilde{\lambda}
an irreducible algebraic representation
\mathsf{L}(\widetilde{\lambda})
, which in turn induces a local system on
\widetilde{X}_{\widetilde{K}^{p}\widetilde{K}_{p}}
or
\overline{\widetilde{X}}_{\widetilde{K}^{p}\widetilde{K}_{p}}
.

The purpose for including these weights and local systems is because the cohomology groups
\mathrm{H}^{\ast}(X_{K^{p}K_{p}},\mathsf{L}(\lambda)),\mathrm{H}^{\ast}(\widetilde{X}_{\widetilde{K}^{p}\widetilde{K}_{p}},\mathsf{L}(\widetilde{\lambda}))
can be understood as spaces of automorphic forms. Fix
\iota:\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}\simeq{\mathbf{C}}
. There are very general theorems due to Franke
[
Fra98
, Theorem 18]
relating the cohomology of symmetric spaces to automorphic forms. For
G
, we merely recall
[
ACC+23
, Theorem 2.4.10]
, which tells us for any
\pi
cuspidal regular algebraic automorphic representation of
\operatorname{GL}_{n}({\mathbb{A}}_{F})
of weight
\iota\lambda
, we have for some good open subgroup
K\subset\operatorname{GL}_{n}({\mathbb{A}}_{F}^{\infty})
the ring map
f:{\mathbf{T}}^{S}\to\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}
associated to the Hecke eigenvalues of
(\iota^{-1}\pi^{\infty})^{K}
factors through
{\mathbf{T}}^{S}\to{\mathbf{T}}^{S}(\mathrm{H}^{\ast}(X_{K^{p}K_{p}},\mathsf{L}(\lambda)))
, the Hecke algebra acting faithfully on
\mathrm{H}^{\ast}(X_{K^{p}K_{p}},\mathsf{L}(\lambda))
. Conversely, if
\mathfrak{m}\subset{\mathbf{T}}^{S}(\mathrm{H}^{\ast}(X_{K^{p}K_{p}},\mathsf{L}(\lambda)))
is non-Eisenstein (see discussion before
Lemma

2.34
) then any ring homomorphism
f:{\mathbf{T}}^{S}(\mathrm{H}^{\ast}(X_{K^{p}K_{p}},\mathsf{L}(\lambda)))_{\mathfrak{m}}\to\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}
comes from a system of Hecke eigenvalues of a regular algebraic cuspidal
\pi
of weight
\iota\lambda
. Moreover, a “purity lemma” of Clozel
[
Clo90
, Lemme 4.9]
tells us that
\mathrm{H}^{\ast}(X_{K^{p}K_{p}},\mathsf{L}(\lambda))_{\mathfrak{m}}\neq 0
implies that there exists a
w\in{\mathbf{Z}}
such that for all
\tau\in\operatorname{Hom}(F,\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})
and
1\leq i\leq n
that
\lambda_{\tau,i}+\lambda_{\tau c,n+1-i}=w
.

As eigenvarieties have weights that vary
p
-adically, we are interested in what possible weights they live over. Namely, let
\mathcal{W}:=\widehat{T(\mathcal{O}_{F^{+},p})}\simeq\widehat{T_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F,p})}
be the rigid space of continuous characters of integral points of the maximal torus. These spaces track the possible weights that
p
-adic automorphic forms can take. Any weight
\lambda\in({\mathbf{Z}}^{n})^{\operatorname{Hom}(F,\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})}\simeq({\mathbf{Z}}^{2n})^{\operatorname{Hom}(F^{+},\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})}
defines an associated point
\lambda\in\mathcal{W}
. We call such points
algebraic
. Note there is an isomorphism
\operatorname{Hom}(F,\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(F\otimes_{{\mathbf{Q}}}{\mathbf{Q}}_{p},\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})=\sqcup_{\nu\in S_{p}(F)}\operatorname{Hom}(F_{\nu},\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})
. Under the isomorphism
{\mathcal{O}}_{F,p}^{\times}\simeq\prod_{\nu\in S_{p}(F)}{\mathcal{O}}_{F_{\nu}}^{\times}
, the character
\lambda\in\mathcal{W}
is given by the formula
(\operatorname{diag}(x_{\nu,1},\dots,x_{\nu,n}))_{\nu}\mapsto\prod_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{\tau\in\operatorname{Hom}(F_{\nu},\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})}\tau(x_{\nu})^{\lambda_{\tau,i}}

Motivated by Clozel’s “purity lemma,” we can then make the following definition.

Definition 2.13
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathcal{W}
be a weight. Then we say
\lambda
is
CTG
(“cohomologically trivial for
G
”) if for all
w\in W^{\overline{P}}\subset W_{\widetilde{G}}
(see
Section

3
for definition) and for all integers
i_{0}\in{\mathbf{Z}}
, there is an embedding
\tau\in\operatorname{Hom}(F,\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})
such that the weight
w\cdot\lambda
satisfies
(w\cdot\lambda)_{\tau}-(w\cdot\lambda)_{\tau\circ c}^{\vee}\neq(i_{0},\dots i_{0})
.
We denote the set of CTG weights by
\mathcal{W}_{CTG}
.

A key reason to care about CTG weights is the following. Continuing to assume that
\mathfrak{m}\subset{\mathbf{T}}^{S}(K^{p})
is non-Eisenstein. Now let
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}:=\mathcal{S}^{\ast}(\mathfrak{m})
. Then if
\widetilde{\lambda}\in({\mathbf{Z}}^{2n})^{\operatorname{Hom}(F^{+},\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})}
is a dominant weight for
\widetilde{G}
which is CTG, then
[
ACC+23
, proof of Theorem 2.4.11]
and the purity lemma implies that
\mathrm{H}^{\ast}(\partial\widetilde{X}_{\widetilde{K}^{p}\widetilde{K}_{p}},\mathsf{L}(\widetilde{\lambda}))_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}=0
in all possible degrees.
Here is a straightforward consequence of the definition of CTG that we will need in the sequel:

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
U\subset\mathcal{W}
be a connected affinoid open. Then
U\cap(\mathcal{W}-\mathcal{W}_{CTG})
is contained in a proper analytic closed
Z\subset U
.

Proof.

By definition,
\delta\in\mathcal{W}-\mathcal{W}_{CTG}
iff for some
w\in W^{\overline{P}}
and
i_{0}\in{\mathbf{Z}}
we have
(w\cdot\delta)_{\tau}-(w\cdot\lambda)_{\tau c}^{\vee}=(i_{0},\dots,i_{0})
. In other words, for all
\tau\in\operatorname{Hom}(F,\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})
and all
1\leq j\leq n
we have
(w\cdot\delta)_{\tau,j}-(w\cdot\delta)_{\tau\circ c,n+1-j}=i_{0}
.
\delta
thus lies one of the
\#W^{\overline{P}}
subvarieties
Z_{w}
defined by the equations
w\cdot\delta)_{\tau,j}-(w\cdot\delta)_{\tau\circ c,n+1-j}=(w\cdot\delta)_{\tau,j^{\prime}}-(w\cdot\delta)_{\tau\circ c,n+1-j^{\prime}}
for all
1\leq j,j^{\prime}\leq n
. Taking
Z:=U\cap\cup_{w\in W^{\overline{P}}}Z_{w}
gives the desired result.
∎

2.14.1
Relevant Hecke operators

In order to discuss Galois representations associated to automorphic data for
G
and
\widetilde{G}
we need to discuss Hecke polynomials, and thus look at certain explicit Hecke operators. For any
1\leq i\leq n
we consider the double coset operators

T_{\nu,i}:=[\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F_{\nu}})\operatorname{diag}(\varpi_{\nu},\dots,\varpi_{\nu},1,\dots,1)\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F_{\nu}})]\in\mathcal{H}(\operatorname{GL}_{n}(F_{\nu}),\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F_{\nu}})),

where there are
i

\varpi_{\nu}
’s along the diagonal. The associated Hecke polynomial is defined as

P_{\nu}(X)=X^{n}-T_{\nu,1}X^{n-1}+\dots+(-1)^{i}q_{\nu}^{i(i-1)/2}T_{\nu,i}X^{n-i}+\dots+q_{\nu}^{n(n-1)/2}T_{\nu,n}\in\mathcal{H}(\operatorname{GL}_{n}(F_{\nu}),\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F_{\nu}}))[X].

Likewise if
\overline{\nu}
is a place of
F^{+}
unramified in
F
and
\nu|\overline{\nu}
and
1\leq i\leq 2n
, there is an associated operator
\widetilde{T}_{\nu,i}\in\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{G}(F_{\overline{\nu}}^{+}),\widetilde{G}(\mathcal{O}_{F_{\overline{\nu}}^{+}}))\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}}{\mathbf{Z}}[q_{\overline{\nu}}^{-1}]
defined in
[
NT16
, Proposition-Definition 5.2]
(denoted
T_{G,\nu,i}
in that source). We can then define another Hecke polynomial:

\widetilde{P}_{\nu}(X):=X^{2n}-\widetilde{T}_{\nu,1}X^{2n-1}+\dots+(-1)^{j}q_{\nu}^{j(j-1)/2}\widetilde{T}_{\nu,j}X^{2n-j}+\dots+q_{\nu}^{n(2n-1)}\widetilde{T}_{\nu,2n}\in(\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{G}(F_{\overline{\nu}}^{+}),\widetilde{G}({\mathcal{O}}_{F_{\overline{\nu}}^{+}}))\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}}{\mathbf{Z}}[q_{\overline{\nu}}^{-1}])[X].

The behavior of the Satake transfer map
\mathcal{S}:\mathcal{H}(\widetilde{G}(F_{\overline{\nu}}^{+}),\widetilde{G}({\mathcal{O}}_{F_{\overline{\nu}}^{+}}))\to\mathcal{H}(\operatorname{GL}_{n}(F_{\nu}),\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F_{\nu}}))
at unramified places can be described using these polynomials. If
f(X)
is a polynomial of degree
d
with invertible constant term
a_{0}
, then denote
f^{\vee}(X):=a_{0}^{-1}X^{d}f(X^{-1})
.

Proposition 2.15
.

Let
\nu
be a place of
F
which is unramified over a place
\overline{\nu}
of
F^{+}
. Then we have

\mathcal{S}(\widetilde{P}_{\nu}(X))=P_{\nu}(X)q_{\nu}^{n(2n-1)}P_{\nu^{c}}^{\vee}(q_{\nu}^{1-2n}X)
.

Proof.

This follows from
[
NT16
, Proposition-Definition 5.3]

∎

With the given setup, we are especially interested in the complexes
\Pi(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}
and

\Pi_{\ast}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}
where
?=\emptyset,c,\partial
, and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}=\mathcal{S}^{\ast}(\mathfrak{m})
. After passing to locally analytic vectors, we now want to study the cohomology
\mathrm{H}^{\ast}(J_{B}(\Pi_{?}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{\operatorname{la}}))
, where we can, for example, take the Jacquet functor with respect to
z=\begin{pmatrix}p^{2n-1}&&&\\
&p^{2n-2}&&\\
&&\ddots&\\
&&&1\end{pmatrix}\in T(F_{p}^{+})^{+}
. We will pass to the dual side and study
\mathrm{H}_{\ast}(J_{B}^{\vee}(C^{?,\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I))))_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}
, via the
U_{2}z
-action.

2.16
Fredholm theory and slopes

There are several approaches to truncating slopes for eigenvarieties, with different advantages. In this subsection, we express the finite slope functors of Emerton (and Fu) on completed (co)homology in terms of Ash–Stevens’s theory of slope decompositions.

We first recall some details about slope decompositions. Let
L/{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
be a finite extension. Let
A
be an affinoid
L
-algebra, and
Q(X)\in A[X]
a polynomial of degree
d.
We say
Q(X)
is
multiplicative
if its leading coefficient is a multiplicative unit in
A
. For any
h\in{\mathbf{Q}}_{\geq 0}
, we say a multiplicative
Q(X)

has slope
\leq h
if for all
x\in\operatorname{Sp}(A)
, the specialization
Q_{x}(X)
has all edges of its Newton polygon having slope
\leq h
. Recall if
Q
is multiplicative with constant term
a_{0}
, we defined
Q^{\vee}(X):=a_{0}^{-1}X^{d}Q(1/X)
. Now if
M
is an
A
-module equipped with a
A
-linear operator
T
, we say an element
m\in M
has slope
\leq h
(with respect to
T
) if there exists a polynomial
Q(X)\in A[X]
of slope
\leq h
such that
Q^{\vee}(T)m=0
. Most crucially, we define a
slope
\leq h
decomposition
of
M
to be an
A[T]
-linear decomposition

M=M_{\leq h}\oplus M_{>h}

such that
M_{\leq h}
is a finitely generated
A
-module and for every multiplicative polynomial
P(X)\in A[X]
of slope
\leq h
, we have
P^{\vee}(T):M_{>h}\xrightarrow{\sim}M_{>h}
is an
A
-linear isomorphism. Such a decomposition is in fact unique. See
[
Han17
, Section 2.3]
for more properties of slope decompositions, including its functorial behavior. For the next lemma, recall that an
A
-Banach module
M
is called
orthonormalizable
(or
ONable
) if there is an isomorphism
A\simeq V\widehat{\otimes}_{L}A
for
V
a
L
-Banach space. Moreover, an
A
-Banach module
M
is called
(Pr)
if there is another
A
-Banach module
Q
such that
M\oplus Q
is ONable.

Lemma 2.17
.

Let
C_{\bullet}
be a bounded complex of (Pr)-Banach modules over an affinoid
{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
-algebra
A
with an
A[u]
-module structure, such that the map
u:\bigoplus_{i}C^{i}\to\bigoplus_{i}C^{i}
is compact
A
-linear endomorphism. Then for any
x\in\operatorname{Sp}(A)
and any
h\in{\mathbf{Q}}_{\geq 0}
there is an affinoid subdomain
\operatorname{Sp}(A^{\prime})\subset\operatorname{Sp}(A)
containing
x
such that
C^{\bullet}\widehat{\otimes}_{A}A^{\prime}
admits a slope
\leq h
decomposition, and
(C^{\bullet}\widehat{\otimes}_{A}A^{\prime})_{\leq h}
is a complex of finite flat
A^{\prime}
-modules.

Proof.

The case where all the
C^{\bullet}
are ONable is
[
Han17
, Proposition 2.3.3]
, which itself is a direct consequence of
[
AS08
, Theorem 4.5.1, Proposition 4.1.2]
.

The general case follows directly: since
\bigoplus_{i}C^{i}
is (Pr), there is an ONable
F
and (Pr)
S
such that
(\bigoplus_{i}C^{i})\oplus S=F
, and we can extend
u
to a compact operator on
F
via the formula
(u,0)
. In particular, by
[
AS08
, Theorem 4.5.1]
there is an affinoid subdomain
\operatorname{Sp}(A^{\prime})\subset\operatorname{Sp}(A)
such that the Fredholm power series for
u
acting on
F\widehat{\otimes}_{A}A^{\prime}
admits a slope
\leq h
factorisation
F(T)=Q(T)\cdot R(T)
in the sense of
loc. cit.
, so that we have a slope
\leq h
decomposition for
F
. Since
u
acts on
S
via zero, the map
(F\widehat{\otimes}_{A}A^{\prime})_{\leq h}\to F\widehat{\otimes}_{A}A^{\prime}\to\left(\bigoplus_{i}C^{i}\right)\widehat{\otimes}_{A}A^{\prime}
is in fact injective, and thus the image of this map gives a definition for slope
\leq h
decomposition
((\bigoplus_{i}C_{i})\widehat{\otimes}_{A}A^{\prime})_{\leq h}
for
(\bigoplus_{i}C_{i})\widehat{\otimes}_{A}A^{\prime}
. As the differentials in
C^{\bullet}
are
u
-equivariant, this gives a slope
\leq h
decomposition for the complex
C^{\bullet}\widehat{\otimes}_{A}A^{\prime}
.
∎

We would like to apply this lemma in the case of complexes computing (dual) Jacquet functors. Since dual Jacquet functors are instead
limits
of Banach spaces, we need to reduce to the case of complexes of Banach modules.

To accomplish this reduction, as in
[
BHS17
, 5.2]
we use the refinement of locally analytic vectors defined in
[
CD14
, 0.3]
. Let
H
be a uniform pro-
p
group. Then for any locally analytic representation
\Pi
of
H
and
h\geq 1
the authors in
loc. cit.
define a subspace
\Pi_{H}^{(h)}
, denoting the space of vectors with, roughly speaking,
(h)
-analytic Mahler expansions with respect to
H
. This space is naturally a Banach representation of
H
. Moreover if
\mathcal{C}^{(h)}(H,L):=(\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{la}}(H,L))_{H}^{h}
, then there are natural compact closed embeddings
\mathcal{C}^{(h)}(H,L)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{(h+1)}(H,L)
, and
\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{la}}(H,L)=\varinjlim\mathcal{C}^{(h)}(H,L)
. Let
D^{(h)}(H,L):=\mathcal{C}^{(h)}(H,L)^{\prime}
. Some facts about these constructions we need are the following
[
CD14
, Theoreme 0.6]
:

1.

\mathcal{C}^{(h)}(T\cap H,L)^{\prime}\simeq D_{<p^{r_{h}}}(T\cap H,L)
, where
r_{h}=\frac{1}{p^{h-1}(p-1)}
.

2.

\Pi_{H}^{(h+1)}=\Pi_{H^{p}}^{(h)}
for any Banach representation
\Pi
of
H
.

Now choose
H\subset I
an open uniform pro-
p
subgroup, such that there is an Iwahori decomposition

H\xrightarrow{\sim}(\overline{N}\cap H)\times(T\cap H)\times(N\cap H)=:\overline{N}_{H}\times T_{H}\times N_{H}.

The Iwahori decomposition induces a topological isomorphism

\mathcal{C}^{(h)}(H,L)\simeq\mathcal{C}^{(h)}(\overline{N}_{H},L)\widehat{\otimes}_{L}\mathcal{C}^{(h)}(T_{H},L)\widehat{\otimes}_{L}\mathcal{C}^{(h)}(N_{H},L).

Proposition 2.18
.

For any weight
\lambda\in\mathcal{W}(\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})
, and
h\in{\mathbf{Q}}_{\geq 0}
, there is an affinoid open neighborhood
\Omega\subset\mathcal{W}
of
\lambda
such that
C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{P}I,D(I)_{N_{0}}\widehat{\otimes}_{D(T_{0})}\mathcal{O}(\Omega))
admits a slope
\leq h
decomposition. More specifically, there is an increasing affinoid open covering
(U_{m})_{m}\subset\mathcal{W}
and complexes of (Pr)-Banach
A_{m}:=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}(U_{m})
-modules
V_{m,\bullet}
such that we have a
T_{0}
-equivariant isomorphism of Fréchet spaces

C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{P}I,D(I)_{N_{0}})\simeq\varprojlim_{m}V_{m,\bullet}

so that the action of
U_{2}z
admits a factorisation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{m+1,\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{m+1,\bullet}\otimes_{A_{m+1}}A_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{m,\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{m+1,\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{m+1,\bullet}\otimes_{A_{m+1}}A_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{m,\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{m+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{m+1}\otimes_{A_{m+1}}1_{A_{m}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{m}}

(6)

and we have isomorphisms
C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I)_{N_{0}}\widehat{\otimes}_{D(T_{0})}\mathcal{O}(\Omega))_{\leq h}\simeq(V_{s,\bullet}\widehat{\otimes}_{A_{s}}\mathcal{O}(\Omega))_{\leq h}
for all
s\gg 0
. Moreover, for any
\Omega^{\prime}\subset\Omega
affinoid open we also have

C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I)_{N_{0}}\widehat{\otimes}_{D(T_{0})}\mathcal{O}(\Omega))_{\leq h}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}(\Omega)}\mathcal{O}(\Omega^{\prime})\simeq C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I)_{N_{0}}\widehat{\otimes}_{D(T_{0})}\mathcal{O}(\Omega^{\prime}))_{\leq h}.

(7)

Moreover, all of these isomorphisms are (up to homotopy)
\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}^{S}
-equivariant, and remain true for Borel-Moore and boundary homology.

Proof.

We heavily rely on
[
BHS17
, Proposition 5.3]
as our guide. First, choose
H\subset I
an open uniform pro-
p
group as above. The proof in
loc. cit.
shows we can write
D(H)_{N_{H}}\simeq\varprojlim W_{m}
where as a
(D(H),A_{m})
-bimodule we have an isomorphism
W_{m}:=(D^{(m)}(H)_{N_{H}})\widehat{\otimes}_{D^{(m)}(T_{H})}B_{m}
, where
B_{m}:=D_{p^{-r_{m}}}(T_{H})
. Moreover, the
W_{m}
are naturally ONable Banach modules over
B_{m}
. Note that as right
B_{m}
-modules we have an isomorphism
W_{m}\simeq D^{(m)}(\overline{N}_{H})\widehat{\otimes}_{L}B_{m}
. Via this isomorphism, we can describe the induced
z
-action as factorizing
z:D^{(m)}(H)_{N_{H}}\to D^{(m)}(z^{-1}\overline{N}_{H}z)\widehat{\otimes}_{L}D^{(m)}(T_{H})\to D^{(m+1)}(\overline{N}_{H})\widehat{\otimes}_{L}D^{(m)}(T_{H})
. With this factorisation in mind, we may get a diagram of bimodules as in (
6
) by defining
\alpha_{m}:=z\otimes 1_{B_{m}}
and
\beta_{m}:D^{(m+1)}(\overline{N}_{H})\widehat{\otimes}_{L}B_{m}\to D^{(m)}(\overline{N}_{H})\widehat{\otimes}_{L}B_{m}
the map induced by
D^{(m+1)}(\overline{N}^{1})\to D^{(m)}(\overline{N}^{1})
. Defining
V_{m}:=C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p})\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}[H]}W_{m}
with its induced Hecke action, we get our desired diagram of complexes. In light of the isomorphism
C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p})\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}[I]}D(I)\simeq C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p})\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}[I]}({\mathbf{Z}}[I]\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}[H]}D(H))\simeq C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p})\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}[H]}D(H)
, these complexes
V_{m}
have a natural action of
T_{0}
on them, since
T_{0}
is abelian, and are thus modules over the algebra
A_{m}:=B_{m}\otimes_{L[T_{H}]}L[T_{0}]
. In fact, the
V_{m}
are naturally (Pr) Banach modules over
A_{m}
, since
A_{m}
is a finite étale
B_{m}
-algebra. Then the Amice transform (applied to
T_{H}
) also implies we have
A_{m}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}(U_{m})
for some affinoid open
U_{m}\subset\mathcal{W}
.

Lemma

2.17
then implies that for any
\lambda\in U_{m}
, there is an affinoid open neighborhood
\Omega\subset U_{m}
containing
\lambda
such that
V_{m}\widehat{\otimes}_{A_{m}}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{W}}(\Omega)
admits a slope
\leq h
decomposition. Likewise, using (
6
) and same proof as
[
Han17
, Proposition 3.1.1]
shows that the characteristic power series
F_{\Omega,s}(X):=\det(1-XU_{z}|V_{s,\bullet}\widehat{\otimes}_{A_{s}}{\mathcal{O}}(\Omega))
satisfies
F_{\Omega,s}(X)=F_{\Omega,s+1}(X)
for all
s\gg 0
. This equality of characteristic power series implies that we can choose a single affinoid neighborhood
\Omega
such that for all
s>0
satisfying
U_{s}\supset\Omega
we have the complexes
V_{s,\bullet}\widehat{\otimes}_{A_{s}}{\mathcal{O}}(\Omega)
admit slope
\leq h
decompositions, and in fact
((C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p})\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}[H]}W_{s})\widehat{\otimes}_{A_{s}}\mathcal{O}(\Omega))_{\leq h}\simeq(C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p})\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}[I]}({\mathbf{Z}}[I]\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}[H]}W_{s})\widehat{\otimes}_{A_{s}}\mathcal{O}(\Omega))_{\leq h}
. For the Fréchet limit
D(I)_{N_{0}}\widehat{\otimes}_{D(T_{0})}{\mathcal{O}}(\Omega)
, if we let
F=P\cdot Q
be a slope
\leq h
-factorisation of the Fredholm series in the sense of
[
Han17
, §2.3]
(in particular,
Q
is a multiplicative polynomial of slope
\leq h
), we can define
(C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I)\widehat{\otimes}_{D(T_{0})}{\mathcal{O}}(\Omega)))_{\leq h}:=\ker Q^{\ast}(\widetilde{U}_{z})
, and using
Lemma

2.19
below deduce we have slope
\leq h
decompositions for
C_{\bullet}(K^{p}I,D(I)_{N_{0}}\widehat{\otimes}\mathcal{O}(\Omega))
and in fact isomorphisms

C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I)_{N_{0}}\widehat{\otimes}\mathcal{O}(\Omega))_{\leq h}\simeq\varprojlim_{m}(C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,W_{m}\widehat{\otimes}_{A_{m}}{\mathcal{O}}(\Omega)))_{\leq h}\simeq C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,V_{s}\widehat{\otimes}_{A_{s}}\mathcal{O}(\Omega))_{\leq h}

for
s\gg 0
. The final statement follows just as in
[
Han17
, Proposition 3.1.4]
.
∎

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
M_{m}:=({\mathbf{Z}}[I]\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}[H]}D^{(m)}(H)_{N_{H}})\widehat{\otimes}_{D^{(m)}(T_{0})}A_{m}
. Then for any
\Omega\subset\mathcal{W}

we have a topological isomorphism of
(D(I),\mathcal{O}(\Omega))
-bimodules
D(I)_{N_{H}}\widehat{\otimes}_{D(T_{0})}\mathcal{O}(\Omega)\simeq\varprojlim_{m}M_{m}\widehat{\otimes}_{A_{m}}\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
. Moreover, the natural
z
-actions on both sides are compatible.

Proof.

First note there are evident maps of
(D(I),\mathcal{O}(\Omega))
-bimodules,
D(I)_{N_{H}}\widehat{\otimes}_{D(T_{0})}\mathcal{O}(\Omega)\to M_{m}\widehat{\otimes}_{A_{m}}\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
, which induces a map
D(I)_{N_{0}}\widehat{\otimes}_{D(T_{0})}\mathcal{O}(\Omega)\to\varprojlim M_{m}\widehat{\otimes}_{A_{m}}\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
. The lemma reduces to showing this map is an isomorphism of topological vector spaces. First note that since
D(T_{0})
is dense in
A_{m}
for any
m\geq 0
, we have a natural topological isomorphism
M_{m}\widehat{\otimes}_{D(T_{0})}\mathcal{O}(\Omega)\simeq M_{m}\widehat{\otimes}_{A_{m}}{\mathcal{O}}(\Omega)
for all
m
. Now by writing
D(I)_{N_{H}}=D(H)_{N_{H}}^{\oplus\#[I:H]}
and
N_{m}:=D^{(m)}(H)_{N_{H}}\widehat{\otimes}_{D^{(m)}(T_{H})}B_{m}
, where
B_{m}:=D_{p^{-r_{h}}}(T_{H})
, we may reduce to showing the map
D(H)_{N_{H}}\widehat{\otimes}_{D(T_{H})}{\mathcal{O}}(\Omega_{H})\to\varprojlim_{m}N_{m}\widehat{\otimes}_{B_{m}}{\mathcal{O}}(\Omega_{H})
, for
\Omega_{H}\subset\widehat{T_{H}}
denoting the image of
\Omega
under
\widehat{T_{0}}\to\widehat{T\cap H}
. Note there is an isomorphism of right
D(T_{H})
-modules
D(H)_{N_{H}}\simeq D(\overline{N}_{H})\widehat{\otimes}_{L}D(T_{H})
and likewise
N_{m}\simeq D^{(m)}(\overline{N}_{H})\widehat{\otimes}_{L}D^{(m)}(T_{H})
, which natural fits into a diagram of right
D(T_{H})
-modules

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(H)_{N_{H}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(\overline{N}_{H})\widehat{\otimes}_{L}D(T_{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{(m)}(\overline{N}_{H})\widehat{\otimes}_{L}D^{(m)}(T_{H})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Taking
-\widehat{\otimes}_{D(T_{H})}\mathcal{O}(\Omega_{H})
of the entire diagram yields

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(H)_{N_{H}}\widehat{\otimes}_{D(T_{H})}\mathcal{O}(\Omega_{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(\overline{N}_{H})\widehat{\otimes}_{L}\mathcal{O}(\Omega_{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{m}\widehat{\otimes}_{B_{m}}\mathcal{O}(\Omega_{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{(m)}(\overline{N}_{H})\widehat{\otimes}_{L}\mathcal{O}(\Omega_{H})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

where for the top line we note
D(\overline{N}_{H})\widehat{\otimes}_{L}D(T_{H})\widehat{\otimes}_{D(T_{H})}\mathcal{O}(\Omega_{H})\simeq D(\overline{N}_{H})\widehat{\otimes}_{L}\mathcal{O}(\Omega_{H})
by associativity of completed tensor product, and we note that likewise that
D(T_{H})
is dense in
D^{(h)}(T_{H})
to get the bottom row. Passing to the limit over
m
in the bottom row then yields

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(H)_{N_{H}}\widehat{\otimes}_{D(T_{H})}\mathcal{O}(\Omega_{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(\overline{N}_{H})\widehat{\otimes}_{L}\mathcal{O}(\Omega_{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varprojlim_{m}N_{m}\widehat{\otimes}_{B_{m}}\mathcal{O}(\Omega_{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varprojlim_{m}D^{(m)}(\overline{N}_{H})\widehat{\otimes}_{L}\mathcal{O}(\Omega_{H})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

But then the right vertical map is the evident isomorphism
D(\overline{N}_{H})\widehat{\otimes}_{L}\mathcal{O}(\Omega_{H})\simeq\varprojlim D^{(m)}(\overline{N}_{H})\widehat{\otimes}_{L}\mathcal{O}(\Omega_{H})
coming from the Fréchet structure, so the left vertical map is also an isomorphism, as desired.
∎

Let
J_{B}^{\vee}(C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I)_{N_{0}}))
denote the dual Jacquet module, with the finite slope part taken with respect to the
U_{2}z
-action. These modules are naturally coadmissible
{\mathcal{O}}_{\widehat{Y}}(\widehat{Y})
-modules (for example, one can use (
6
)!), and write
\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\operatorname{BS}}
for the associated coherent sheaves. Let
\mathcal{Z}_{i}:=\operatorname{supp}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\widehat{Y}}(\widehat{Y})}\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\operatorname{BS}}
be the support in degree
i
. Now let
U_{\leq h}=\{p^{-h}\leq|z|\leq p^{h}\}\subset{\mathbf{G}}_{m}
be the closed annulus of radius
p^{-h}
. As in
[
BHS17
, Lemme 3.10]
, we also have the following consequence.

Corollary 2.20
.

For each
i
,
\mathcal{Z}_{i}
is a Fredholm hypersurface, and there is admissible affinoid cover
(U_{j})_{j}
of
\mathcal{Z}_{i}
such that the projection
\pi_{\mathcal{W}}:U_{j}\to W_{j}\subset\mathcal{W}
is a finite surjective map onto an affinoid open, and
\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\operatorname{BS}}(U_{j})
is a finite projective
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{W}}(W_{j})
-module.

Proof.

Given
Proposition

2.18
, this follows from the same proof as in
[
BHS17
, Lemme 3.10]
. Moreover, note that given
\lambda\in\mathcal{W}(\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})
, we could even take
U_{i}=\operatorname{supp}\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\operatorname{BS}}\cap(\Omega\times U_{\leq h})
, where
\Omega
is an affinoid over which a slope
\leq h
decomposition exists, since the slope
\leq h
part of a (Pr)-module is finite projective over
{\mathcal{O}}(\Omega)
.
∎

We now show how Emerton’s finite slope part relates to slope decompositions.

Lemma 2.21

(Comparing finite slope parts)
.

Suppose we are in the setting of
Proposition

2.18
, so that
\Omega\subset\mathcal{W}
is an affinoid open and
h\in{\mathbf{Q}}_{>0}
is such that
C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I)_{N_{0}}\widehat{\otimes}_{D(T_{0})}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{W}}(\Omega))
admits a slope
\leq h
decomposition. Then we have a
T(F_{p}^{+})
and
\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}^{S}
-equivariant isomorphism

\mathrm{H}_{\ast}(J_{B}^{\vee}(C^{BS}(K^{p}I,D(I))))\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\widehat{Y}}(\mathcal{W}\times{\mathbf{G}}_{m})}(\mathcal{O}(\Omega)\widehat{\otimes}_{L}\mathcal{O}(U_{\leq h}))\simeq\mathrm{H}_{\ast}(C^{BS}(K^{p}I,D(I)_{N_{0}}\widehat{\otimes}_{D(T_{0})}\mathcal{O}(\Omega))_{\leq h}).

Proof.

We need to more explicitly compute
C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I)_{N_{0}})_{\operatorname{fs}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{W}\times\widehat{{\mathbf{G}}}_{m})}(\mathcal{O}(\Omega)\widehat{\otimes}\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbf{G}}_{m}}(U_{\leq h}))
. Noting the Amice transform isomorphism
D(T_{0})\simeq\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathcal{W})
, we also then have
{\mathcal{O}}_{\widehat{Y}}(\mathcal{W}\times\widehat{{\mathbf{G}}}_{m})\simeq D(T_{0})\widehat{\otimes}_{L}L\{\{z^{\pm 1}\}\}
. Then we can write

\displaystyle J_{B}^{\vee}(C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I)))\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\widehat{Y}}}(\mathcal{O}(\Omega)\widehat{\otimes}_{L}\mathcal{O}(U_{\leq h}))

\displaystyle\simeq(C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I)_{N_{0}})\widehat{\otimes}_{L[z]}L\{\{z^{\pm 1}\}\})\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{W}\times\widehat{{\mathbf{G}}}_{m})}(\mathcal{O}(\Omega)\widehat{\otimes}L\langle p^{h}z,p^{h}z^{-1}\rangle)

\displaystyle\simeq(C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I)_{N_{0}})\widehat{\otimes}_{D(T_{0})}\mathcal{O}(\Omega))\widehat{\otimes}_{L[z]}(L\{\{z^{\pm 1}\}\}\widehat{\otimes}_{L\{\{z^{\pm 1}\}\}}L\langle p^{h}z,p^{h}z^{-1}\rangle)

\displaystyle\simeq C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I)_{N_{0}}\widehat{\otimes}_{D(T_{0})}{\mathcal{O}}(\Omega))\widehat{\otimes}_{L[z]}L\langle p^{h}z,p^{h}z^{-1}\rangle.

Now on
\Omega
we have that by
[
AS08
, Proposition 4.5.1]
(as is used in
Lemma

2.17
) the Fredholm power series admits a slope
\leq h
factorisation
F_{\Omega}(X)=QR
, where
Q
is a multiplicative polynomial of of slope
\leq h
. Then in fact the proof of
[
BHS17
, Lemme 3.10]
implies the space
C^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(I)_{N_{0}}\widehat{\otimes}_{D(T_{0})}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{W}}(\Omega))\widehat{\otimes}_{L[z]}L\langle p^{h}z,p^{h}z^{-1}\rangle
is a projective module over
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}(\Omega)
which is precisely the locus on which
Q^{\ast}(U_{z})=0
. Recalling from
[
Han17
, 2.2]
that a factorization
P=QR
of the Fredholm series yields a description
M_{\leq h}:=\{m\in M:Q^{\vee}(U_{z})=0\}
, we have our desired isomorphism.
∎

2.22
An exact sequence on Jacquet functors

We can now apply this formalism to prove the main useful tools we need for the Jacquet functors in the CM fields setting. The first goal is to use slope decompositions to get a long exact sequence on Jacquet functors.

Lemma 2.23
.

Let
X
be quasi-Stein rigid space equipped with an admissible affinoid open cover
X=\cup_{i}U_{i}
. Then a sequence
\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{N}\to\mathcal{P}
of coherent sheaves on
X
is exact if and only for all
i
the sequence
\Gamma(U_{i},\mathcal{M})\to\Gamma(U_{i},\mathcal{N})\to\Gamma(U_{i},\mathcal{P})
is exact.

Proof.

Since
X
is quasi-Stein,
\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{N}\to\mathcal{P}
is exact iff
\Gamma(X,\mathcal{M})\to\Gamma(X,\mathcal{N})\to\Gamma(X,\mathcal{P})
is exact. On the other hand, exactness of sheaves can be checked locally, so
\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{N}\to\mathcal{P}
is exact iff
\mathcal{M}|_{U_{i}}\to\mathcal{N}|_{U_{i}}\to\mathcal{P}|_{U_{i}}
is exact as coherent sheaves on
U_{i}
. Since
U_{i}
is affinoid,
\mathcal{M}|_{U_{i}}\to\mathcal{N}|_{U_{i}}\to\mathcal{P}|_{U_{i}}
is exact iff
\Gamma(U_{i},\mathcal{M})\to\Gamma(U_{i},\mathcal{N})\to\Gamma(U_{i},\mathcal{P})
is exact, proving the lemma.
∎

Specializing the previous formalism to the case of quasi-split unitary group yields the following result.

Lemma 2.24
.

Let
G=\widetilde{G}/{\mathcal{O}}_{F^{+}}
be the quasi-split unitary group. Let
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}\subset\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}^{S}
be maximal ideal in the support of
\mathrm{H}^{\ast}(\widetilde{X}_{\widetilde{K}^{p}\widetilde{K}_{p}},\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p})
for some good level
\widetilde{K}^{p}\widetilde{K}_{p}
, such that
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}
is decomposed generic in the sense of
Definition

5.2
.
Then we have a
\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}^{S}
-equivariant short exact sequence of essentially admissible
T(F_{p}^{+})
-representations

0\to\mathrm{H}^{d-1}(J_{B}(\Pi_{\partial}(\widetilde{K}^{p})^{\operatorname{la}}))_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}\to\mathrm{H}^{d}(J_{B}(\Pi_{c}(\widetilde{K}^{p})^{\operatorname{la}}))_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}\to\mathrm{H}^{d}(J_{B}(\Pi(\widetilde{K}^{p})^{\operatorname{la}}))_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}\to\mathrm{H}^{d}(J_{B}(\Pi_{\partial}(\widetilde{K}^{p})^{\operatorname{la}}))_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}\to 0.

Proof.

First we claim there is a
\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}^{S}
-equivariant exact sequence of maps of essentially admissible
T(F_{p}^{+})
-representations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{d-1}(J_{B}(\Pi(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{\operatorname{la}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{d-1}(J_{B}(\Pi_{\partial}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{\operatorname{la}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{d}(J_{B}(\Pi_{c}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{\operatorname{la}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{d}(J_{B}(\Pi(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{\operatorname{la}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{d}(J_{B_{p}}(\Pi_{\partial}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{\operatorname{la}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{d+1}(J_{B}(\Pi(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{\operatorname{la}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}

Given such a sequence, the result would follow by showing
\mathrm{H}^{d-1}(J_{B}(\Pi(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{\operatorname{la}})),\mathrm{H}^{d+1}(J_{B}(\Pi_{c}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{\operatorname{la}}))=0
. Indeed, both claims follow from, for example
[
Fu22b
, Lemma 5.4]
and the torsion vanishing statements in
Theorem

5.3
, due to Caraiani–Scholze and Koshikawa. The point is that
\mathrm{H}^{d-1}(\widetilde{X}_{\widetilde{K}^{p}\widetilde{K}_{p}},{\mathbb{F}}_{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}=0
and
\mathrm{H}_{c}^{d+1}(\widetilde{X}_{\widetilde{K}^{p}\widetilde{K}_{p}},{\mathbb{F}}_{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}=0
imply a similar vanishing statement for any coefficient system admitting a suitable
{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}
-lattice. We now construct the above maps, and show the sequence is exact.

By dualizing it suffices to show we have a short exact sequence of coherent sheaves on
\widehat{T(F_{p}^{+})}

\dots\to M_{\partial}^{d}\to M^{d}\to M_{c}^{d}\to M_{\partial}^{d-1}\to\dots.

The maps
M_{\partial}^{d}\to M^{d}\to M_{c}^{d}
are naturally induced by maps of complexes, but we have not yet defined the boundary maps
M_{c}^{i}\to M_{\partial}^{i-1}
. Let
\mathcal{Z}
be the union of the Fredholm hypersurfaces
\mathcal{Z}_{i}^{\operatorname{BS}},\mathcal{Z}_{i}^{\partial,\operatorname{BS}},\mathcal{Z}_{i}^{\operatorname{BM},\operatorname{BS}}
from
Corollary

2.20
, which is again a Fredholm hypersurface. Note that all the modules in the sequence are coadmissible over
\mathcal{Z}\subset\widehat{Y}
, a quasi-Stein space. Thus to define such a map
M_{c}^{d}\to M_{\partial}^{d-1}
, it suffices to define maps compatibly on an admissible open covering of
\mathcal{Z}
. Moreover,
Lemma

2.23
tells us it suffices to check exactness on an admissible open covering of
\mathcal{Z}
. But taking any
x\in\mathcal{Z}
, and any
h\geq 0
, we can choose an affinoid open neighborhood
\Omega\subset\mathcal{W}
such that all modules involved admit slope
\leq h
decompositions over
U_{h,\Omega}:=\mathcal{Z}\cap(\Omega\times U_{\leq h})
. In particular, using
Corollary

2.10
and
Lemma

2.21
over
U_{h,\Omega}
, our sequence amounts to showing there is a sequence of the form

\mathrm{H}_{d}(C_{\bullet}^{\partial}(K^{p}I,D(\Omega)))_{\leq h}\to\mathrm{H}_{d}(C_{\bullet}(K^{p}I,D(\Omega)))_{\leq h}\to\mathrm{H}_{d}(C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BM}}(K^{p}I,D(\Omega)))_{\leq h}\to\mathrm{H}_{d-1}(C_{\bullet}^{\partial}(K^{p}I,D(\Omega)))_{\leq h}

(8)

is exact.
However, note that there is a sequence of maps

\dots\to\mathrm{H}_{d}(C_{\bullet}^{\partial}(K^{p}I,D(\Omega)))\to\mathrm{H}_{d}(C_{\bullet}(K^{p}I,D(\Omega)))\to\mathrm{H}_{d}(C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BM}}(K^{p}I,D(\Omega)))\to\mathrm{H}_{d-1}(C_{\bullet}^{\partial}(K^{p}I,D(\Omega)))\to\dots

(9)

arising from the distinguished triangle

0\to C_{\bullet}^{\partial}(K^{p}I,D(\Omega))\to C_{\bullet}(K^{p}I,D(\Omega))\to C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BM}}(K^{p}I,D(\Omega))\to 0,

and is thus exact
2
2

2

We thank Christian Johansson for noting we can apply slope decompositions
after
passing to cohomology.
. The maps on cohomology are equivariant for the action of
T^{+}
, and slope
\leq h
decompositions are functorial direct summands, and are thus exact. So both the existence of the maps
M_{c}^{d}\to M_{\partial}^{d-1}
and the exactness of (
8
) follow from (
9
). The vanishing of the degrees beyond
d
and below
d+1
follow after localising at
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}
.

∎

2.25
Small slope classicality and torsionness of the boundary

We can now prove
Theorem

1.5
. For this entire section we work with
\widetilde{G}/{\mathcal{O}}_{F^{+}}
, even though many of the intermediate lemmas work more generally.

For any continuous character
\delta:T_{0}\to L^{\times}
, we denote standard action of
z
on
\operatorname{Ind}_{B_{0}}^{I}\lambda
by
z_{\operatorname{std}}
, i.e. the action induced by conjugation
\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{la}}(\overline{N}^{1},\lambda)\xrightarrow{\operatorname{res}}\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{la}}(z^{-1}\overline{N}^{1}z,\lambda)\xrightarrow{\operatorname{conj}_{z}}\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{la}}(\overline{N}^{1},\lambda)
. For any
w\in W_{\widetilde{G}}
we equip
\operatorname{Ind}_{B_{0}}^{I}w_{0}(w\cdot\lambda)
with the left
T^{+}
action given by
z\ast_{w}f:=(w_{0}\lambda)(z)\cdot(w_{0}(w\cdot\lambda))^{-1}(z)\cdot(z_{\operatorname{std}}f)
. Now let
\lambda\in\mathcal{W}
be a dominant algebraic weight, so that
w_{0}\lambda
is anti-dominant. Then let
\operatorname{Ind}_{B_{0}}^{I,\operatorname{alg}}w_{0}\lambda\simeq\mathsf{L}(\lambda)
be the algebraic induction of
w_{0}\lambda
, which is isomorphic to the irreducible algebraic representation of highest weight
\lambda
.
\mathsf{L}(\lambda)
may also be viewed as an irreducible algebraic representation of
I_{m}
for any open subgroup
I_{m}\subset I
. Likewise, we let
L_{\operatorname{lalg}}(\lambda)=(\operatorname{Ind}_{B_{0}}^{I}w_{0}\lambda)^{\operatorname{lalg}}
denote the locally algebraic induction, which naturally has an inclusion
\mathsf{L}(\lambda)\hookrightarrow L_{\operatorname{lalg}}(\lambda)
.

Theorem 2.26

(Jones
[
Jon11
, Theorem 26]
)
.

We have an exact sequence of admissible locally analytic
I
-representations, equivariant for
\ast_{w}
-actions of
T^{+}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\to L_{\operatorname{lalg}}(\lambda)\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Ind}_{B_{0}}^{I}w_{0}\lambda\to\bigoplus_{\ell(w)=1}\operatorname{Ind}_{B_{0}}^{I}(w_{0}(w\cdot\lambda))\to\bigoplus_{\ell(w)=2}\operatorname{Ind}_{B_{0}}^{I}(w_{0}(w\cdot\lambda))\to\dots.}

Passing to the dual side yields an exact sequence of
D(I)
-modules.

\dots\to\bigoplus_{\ell(w)=1}D(I)\otimes_{D(B_{0})}w_{0}(w\cdot\lambda)\to D(I)\otimes_{D(B_{0})}w_{0}\lambda\to(L_{\operatorname{lalg}}(\lambda))^{\vee}\to 0.

Note that we have a surjective map
(L_{\operatorname{lalg}}(\lambda))^{\vee}\to\mathsf{L}(\lambda)^{\vee}
, where
\mathsf{L}(\lambda)^{\vee}
is isomorphic to the irreducible algebraic representation of highest weight
-w_{0}\lambda
.
The (expected) punchline is that the homology of
(L_{\operatorname{lalg}}(\lambda))^{\vee}
is related to cohomology of algebraic representations, and thus classical automorphic forms.

Lemma 2.27
.

We have an Hecke-equivariant isomorphism

\mathrm{H}_{\ast}(C^{\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I,L_{\operatorname{lalg}}(\lambda)^{\vee}))^{\vee}\simeq\varinjlim_{\widetilde{K}_{p}\supset B_{0}}\mathrm{H}^{\ast}(\widetilde{X}_{\widetilde{K}^{p}\widetilde{K}_{p}},\mathsf{L}(\lambda)).

Moreover, for any
h\in{\mathbf{Q}}_{\geq 0}
we also have

\mathrm{H}_{\ast}(C^{\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I,L_{\operatorname{lalg}}(\lambda)^{\vee})_{\leq h})^{\vee}\simeq\mathrm{H}^{\ast}(\widetilde{X}_{\widetilde{K}^{p}I},\mathsf{L}(\lambda))_{\leq h}.

Analogous results hold for compactly supported and boundary cohomology.

Proof.

Essentially by definition

\mathrm{H}_{\ast}(C_{\partial}^{\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I,L_{\operatorname{alg}}(\lambda)^{\vee}))^{\vee}
computes
\mathrm{H}^{\ast}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I,L_{\operatorname{lalg}}(\lambda)).

Writing
L_{\operatorname{lalg}}(\lambda)=\varinjlim_{m}L_{\operatorname{m}-\operatorname{alg}}(\lambda)
, where
L_{\operatorname{m}-\operatorname{alg}}(\lambda)\simeq\operatorname{Ind}_{I_{m}}^{I}\mathsf{L}(\lambda)
, and
I_{m}\subset I
is a cofinal system of open subgroups containing
B_{0}
. Then Shapiro’s lemma (see, for example,
[
NT16
, Corollary 2.21]
) implies
\mathrm{H}^{\ast}(\widetilde{X}_{\widetilde{K}^{p}I},\operatorname{Ind}_{I_{m}}^{I}\mathsf{L}(\lambda))\simeq\mathrm{H}^{\ast}(\widetilde{X}_{\widetilde{K}^{p}I_{m}},\mathsf{L}(\lambda))
, and thus

\mathrm{H}^{\ast}(\widetilde{X}_{\widetilde{K}^{p}I},L_{\operatorname{lalg}}(\lambda))=\varinjlim\mathrm{H}^{\ast}(\widetilde{X}_{\widetilde{K}^{p}I},L_{m-\operatorname{alg}}(\lambda))\simeq\varinjlim\mathrm{H}^{\ast}(\widetilde{X}_{\widetilde{K}^{p}I_{m}},\mathsf{L}(\lambda)).

By the first isomorphism, the second isomorphism reduces to showing that the natural maps
\mathrm{H}^{\ast}(\widetilde{X}_{\widetilde{K}^{p}I_{m}},\mathsf{L}(\lambda))_{\leq h}\to\mathrm{H}^{\ast}(\widetilde{X}_{\widetilde{K}^{p}I_{m+1}},\mathsf{L}(\lambda))_{\leq h}
are isomorphisms. This claim follows from a variant of
Proposition

2.18
(see e.g.
[
Urb11
, Lemma 4.3.6]
).
∎

We then get to our desired torsion-ness statement via two spectral sequences. For
y\in\mathcal{W}(\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})
, let
D(\lambda):=D(I)\otimes_{D(B_{0})}y
.

Lemma 2.28
.

We have a Hecke-equivariant,
z
-equivariant spectral sequence of
\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
-modules:

E_{p,q}^{2}=\operatorname{Tor}_{p}^{\mathcal{O}(\Omega)}(\mathrm{H}_{q}(C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I,D(\Omega))_{\leq h}),\lambda)\implies\mathrm{H}_{p+q}(C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I,D(\lambda)))_{\leq h},

and analogous sequences for Borel-Moore and boundary homology.

Proof.

The proof is essentially identical to
[
Han17
, Theorem 3.3.1]
, but with slightly different notation: this spectral sequence arises from the hyperhomology for the right exact functor
(-)\otimes_{\mathcal{O}(\Omega)}\lambda,
but with several additional observations to get the slope-
\leq h
decompositions and Hecke and
T^{+}
-equivariance.
Consider the hypertor group
\mathbf{Tor}_{p+q}^{\mathcal{O}(\Omega)}(C_{\bullet}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I,D(\Omega)),\lambda))
. Since
(C_{\bullet}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I,D(\Omega))
is a complex of
\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}^{S}
-modules with
T^{+}
-action, we have a natural
\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}^{S}
and
T^{+}
-action on this hyperhomology group. This group also admits a slope
\leq h
decomposition, which can also be computed as

\mathrm{H}_{p+q}(C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(\Omega))_{\leq h}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}(\Omega)}\lambda)=\mathrm{H}_{p+q}(C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(\lambda))_{\leq h})
, where we are using that
C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(K^{p}I,D(\Omega))_{\leq h}
is complex of finite projective
\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
-modules. The hyperhomology spectral sequence gives a sequence of
\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
-modules

E_{pq}^{2}=\operatorname{Tor}_{p}^{\mathcal{O}(\Omega)}(\mathrm{H}_{q}(C_{\bullet}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I,D(\Omega)))_{\leq h},\lambda)\implies\mathrm{H}_{p+q}(C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I,D(\lambda))_{\leq h}),

which is naturally equivariant for the
\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}^{S}
and
T^{+}
-actions.

∎

For the next lemma, for any
w\in W_{\widetilde{G}}
and
h\in{\mathbf{Q}}_{>0}
, set
h(\lambda,w):=h-v_{p}((w\cdot\lambda)(w_{0}z))+v_{p}(\lambda(w_{0}z))
.

Lemma 2.29
.

For any
w\in W_{\widetilde{G}}
and
h\in{\mathbf{Q}}_{>0}
we have a Hecke and
T^{+}
-equivariant spectral sequence

E_{1}^{p,q}=\bigoplus_{\ell(w)=q}\mathrm{H}_{p}(C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I,D(w_{0}(w\cdot\lambda))))_{\leq h(\lambda,w)}\implies\mathrm{H}_{p+q}(C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I,\mathsf{L}(\lambda)^{\vee}))_{\leq h},

and analogous sequences for Borel–Moore and boundary homology.

Proof.

This is essentially
[
Urb11
, Theorem 4.4.1]
in our context. We recall the construction for completeness. Since
C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I)
is a complex of finite free
{\mathbf{Z}}[I]
-modules, tensoring the BGG resolution from
Theorem

2.26
with
C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I)
yields a double complex with exact rows

0\to C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p})\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}[I]}D(w_{0}(w_{0}\cdot\lambda))\to\dots\to C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p})\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}[I]}D(w_{0}(\lambda))\to C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p})\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}[I]}L_{\operatorname{lalg}}(\lambda)^{\vee}\to 0.

Equipping each term with its
U_{2}z
-action, this double complex is in fact
U_{2}z
equivariant. The entire complex has slope
\leq h
-decompositions. Unraveling the slope conditions for the
\ast_{w}
-actions gives a double complex with exact rows

\dots\to\bigoplus_{\ell(w)=1}C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I,D(w_{0}(w\cdot\lambda)))_{\leq h(\lambda,w)}\to(C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I,D(w_{0}\lambda))_{\leq h}\to(C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I),L_{\operatorname{lalg}}(\lambda)^{\vee})_{\leq h}\to 0.

In particular, the total homology in degree
i
of the double complex

0\to C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I,D(w_{0}(w_{0}\cdot\lambda)))_{\leq h(\lambda,w_{0})}\to\dots\to C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I,D(\lambda))_{\leq h}

is isomorphic to
\mathrm{H}_{\bullet}(C^{{\operatorname{BS}}}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I,\mathsf{L}(\lambda)^{\vee}))_{\leq h}
by
Lemma

2.27
, since taking first homology of the columns degenerates by the BGG resolution and passing to slope
\leq h
parts is also exact. Passing first to homology of the columns yields the first page of a spectral sequence with
E_{p,q}^{1}\simeq\bigoplus_{\ell(w)=q}H_{p}(C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I,D(w\cdot\lambda))_{\leq h(\lambda,w)}
, as desired.
∎

We say
h
is a
small slope for

\lambda
if for all
w\in W_{\widetilde{G}}\setminus\{1\}
we have
h<v_{p}((w\cdot\lambda)(w_{0}z))-v_{p}(\lambda(w_{0}z))
. Equivalently,
h(\lambda,w)<0
for all
w\neq 1
.

Corollary 2.30

(Small slope classicality)
.

If
h
is a small slope for
\lambda
then we have a Hecke-equivariant isomorphism

\mathrm{H}_{p}(C^{BS}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I,D(w_{0}\lambda)))_{\leq h}\simeq\mathrm{H}_{p}(C^{BS}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I,\mathsf{L}(\lambda)^{\vee}))_{\leq h},

as well as analogous statements for Borel-Moore and boundary homology.

Proof.

By
Lemma

2.29
it suffices to show that for
w\neq 1
that
\mathrm{H}_{p}(C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I,D(w_{0}(w\cdot\lambda)))_{\leq h(\lambda,w)}=0
. All that is needed is that the action of
z
stabilizes a
{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}
-lattice of
V_{p}:=\mathrm{H}_{p}(C_{\bullet}^{\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I,D(w_{0}(w\cdot\lambda)))
. Indeed, such a fact would imply that the
U_{2}z
-operator must only have nonnegative slopes, and if
h
is small slope then
h(\lambda,w)<0
for all
w\neq 1
, so that
(V_{p})_{\leq h(\lambda,w)}=0
.

For showing the stability of a lattice, note that the homotopy equivalence on
C^{\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p})

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C(\widetilde{K}^{p})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
is defined integrally, and thus the right
z
-action is also defined integrally. Likewise, it is clear the
z
-action on the Banach module
D^{(s)}(I)\widehat{\otimes}_{D^{(s)}(B_{0})}\lambda
preserves the unit ball of this Banach space, since on the dual side the map is induced by a restriction map
C^{(s)}(\overline{N}^{1}T_{0},L)\to C^{(s)}(z^{-1}\overline{N}^{1}zT_{0},L)
, which does not increase the norm. In particular,
C^{\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p})\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}[I]}(D^{(s)}(I)\widehat{\otimes}_{D^{(s)}(B_{0})}\lambda)^{\operatorname{Norm}\leq 1}
is stable under the
U_{2}z
-action, and is a
{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}
-lattice of
C^{(s)}:=C^{\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p})\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}[I]}(D^{(s)}(I)\widehat{\otimes}_{D^{(s)}(B_{0})}w_{0}(w\cdot\lambda))
, and so
H_{p}(C^{(s)})_{\leq h(\lambda,w)}\simeq\mathrm{H}_{p}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I,D(w_{0}(w\cdot\lambda)))_{\leq h(\lambda,w)}=0
for
s\gg 0
and
h
a small slope for
\lambda
.
∎

Corollary 2.31
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}=\operatorname{supp}_{\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{Y}}(\widehat{Y})}(J_{B}^{\vee}(C_{\bullet}^{\partial,\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I,D(I)_{N_{0}})))
. For any
i
, let
V_{i}:=(\mathrm{H}^{i}(J_{B}(\Pi_{\partial}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{\operatorname{la}})))^{\prime}
. Then let
U_{h,\Omega}:=\mathcal{Z}\cap(\Omega\times U_{\leq h})
, where
\Omega
is chosen so that over
\Omega
,
C_{\bullet}^{\partial,\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p},D(I)_{N_{0}}\widehat{\otimes}_{D(T_{0})}{\mathcal{O}}(\Omega))
admits a slope
\leq h
decomposition. Then
V_{i}(U_{h,\Omega})
is a finitely generated torsion module over
\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
.

Proof.

By the above comparison theorems we have
V_{i}(U_{h,\Omega})=\mathrm{H}_{i}(C^{\partial,\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I,D(I)_{N_{0}}\widehat{\otimes}_{D(T_{0})}{\mathcal{O}}(\Omega)))_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}},\leq h}=:H_{i}
. We first prove torsion-ness in the case
\Omega
contains a an algebraic weight. Thus by
Lemma

2.14
there is an accumulating set of dominant algebraic weights which are CTG in the sense of
Definition

2.13
, and by the same argument we can in fact choose
w_{0}\lambda\in\Omega
for
\lambda
a dominant CTG algebraic weight.
Applying

Lemma

2.28
gives a spectral sequence

E_{i,j}^{2}=\operatorname{Tor}_{i}^{\mathcal{O}(\Omega)}(\mathrm{H}_{j},w_{0}\lambda)\implies\mathrm{H}_{i+j}(C^{\partial,\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I,D(w_{0}\lambda)))_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}},\leq h}.

When
h
is a small slope for
\lambda
,
Corollary

2.30
gives an isomorphism

\mathrm{H}_{i+j}(C^{\partial,\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I,D(w_{0}\lambda)))_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}},\leq h}\simeq\mathrm{H}_{i+j}(\partial{\widetilde{X}}_{\widetilde{K}^{p}I},\mathsf{L}(\lambda)^{\vee})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}},\leq h}.

But then by
Lemma

2.27
and the previously cited
[
ACC+23
, Proof of 2.4.11]
that
\mathrm{H}_{i+j}(\partial{\widetilde{X}}_{\widetilde{K}^{p}I},\mathsf{L}(\lambda)^{\vee})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}=0
if
\lambda
is CTG.

We claim that all the
\mathrm{H}_{i}
are not supported at
w_{0}\lambda
, and are thus torsion. Let
\mathfrak{p}_{w_{0}\lambda}\subset{\mathcal{O}}(\Omega)
be the prime ideal corresponding to the point
w_{0}\lambda
. For contradiction, let
j
be the smallest degree such that
\operatorname{supp}_{\mathcal{O}(\Omega)}\mathrm{H}_{j}\ni\mathfrak{p}_{w_{0}\lambda}
, i.e.
\mathrm{H}_{j,\mathfrak{p}_{w_{0}\lambda}}\neq 0
. Then by Nakayama’s lemma we have
\mathrm{H}_{j}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}(\Omega)}w_{0}\lambda\neq 0
, and for all
k<j
and
d\geq 0
, so that that
\operatorname{Tor}_{d}^{\mathcal{O}(\Omega)}(\mathrm{H}_{k},w_{0}\lambda)\simeq(\operatorname{Tor}_{d}^{\mathcal{O}(\Omega)}(\mathrm{H}_{k},\lambda))_{\mathfrak{p}_{w_{0}\lambda}}\simeq\operatorname{Tor}_{d}^{\mathcal{O}(\Omega)}((\mathrm{H}_{k})_{\mathfrak{p}_{w_{0}\lambda}},w_{0}\lambda)=0
. Thus, the term
\mathrm{H}_{j}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}(\Omega)}w_{0}\lambda
survives to the
E_{\infty}
-page of the spectral sequence, implying
\mathrm{H}_{j}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}(\Omega)}w_{0}\lambda
is identified with a submodule of
\mathrm{H}_{j}(C^{\partial,\operatorname{BS}}(\widetilde{K}^{p}I,D(w_{0}\lambda)))_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}},\leq h}=0
by the previous discussion, giving a contradiction. So no such
j
exists, and all the
\mathrm{H}_{j}
are torsion over
\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
, provided
\Omega
contains a locally algebraic weight.

The case where
\Omega
does not contain a locally algebraic weight follows exactly as Step 2 of
[
Han17
, proof of Thm 4.4.1]
.
∎

2.32
Eigenvarieties, and their associated Galois representations

We recall the definitions of the eigenvarieties we need for our purposes. Recall an
eigenvariety datum
(
[
Fu22b
, Definition 6.1]
) is a tuple
D=(T,\mathcal{M},{\mathbf{T}},\psi)
where
T
is a topologically finitely generated abelian locally
{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
-analytic group,
\mathcal{M}
a coherent sheaf on the space of characters
\widehat{T}
,
{\mathbf{T}}
a commutative
{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}
-algebra, and
\psi:{\mathbf{T}}\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}\to\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{O}(\widehat{T})}(\mathcal{M})
a
{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
-algebra homomorphism. Associated to this data we can form a variety
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}(D)
, defined by the relative Spec of
\mathcal{A}
over
\widehat{T}
, where
\mathcal{A}
is the
\mathcal{O}(\widehat{T})
-algebra generated by the image of the map
\psi
. Since
\mathcal{M}
is a coherent sheaf, the natural projection map
\mathcal{E}\to\widehat{T}
(which is a map of rigid analytic spaces) is finite. Very concretely, the points
\mathcal{E}(\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})
is the collection of points
(x,\delta)\in\operatorname{Hom}({\mathbf{T}},\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})\times\widehat{T}(\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})
such that
\operatorname{Hom}_{T}(\delta,\mathcal{M}[x])\neq 0
(see
[
Fu22b
, Proposition 6.2]
). In practice,
T
is the points of a maximal torus of a reductive group,
\mathcal{M}
is the dual of a derived Jacquet module,
{\mathbf{T}}
is a spherical Hecke algebra, and
\psi:{\mathbf{T}}\to\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{O}(\widehat{T})}(M)
comes from an action on a locally symmetric space. Moreover, suppose that in fact
{\mathbf{T}}
is a complete Noetherian local
{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}
-algebra. Then we can form an analogous construction of an
\mathcal{E}
as a subspace of
(\operatorname{Spf}{\mathbf{T}}^{\operatorname{red}})^{\operatorname{rig}}\times\widehat{T}
, where
(-)^{\operatorname{rig}}
denotes the rigid generic fibre (see
[
dJ95
, §0.2]
), and
{\mathbf{T}}^{\operatorname{red}}
denotes the (algebraic) reduction of
{\mathbf{T}}
.
3
3

3

Taking the reduced quotient is only needed for the global Galois representation on the eigenvariety for
G
.

We apply this formalism to
G
and
\widetilde{G}
. Namely, we consider the following eigenvariety data

\displaystyle D^{n}(G)_{\mathfrak{m}}

\displaystyle=(\widehat{T_{n}(F_{p})},\mathrm{H}^{n}(J_{B}(\Pi(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\operatorname{la}}))^{\prime},{\mathbf{T}}^{S}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}},\psi_{\mathfrak{m},n})

\displaystyle D^{n}(\widetilde{G})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}

\displaystyle=(\widehat{T(F_{p}^{+})},\mathrm{H}^{n}(J_{B}(\Pi(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{\operatorname{la}}))^{\prime},\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}^{S}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}},\psi_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}},n})

\displaystyle D_{c}^{n}(\widetilde{G})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}

\displaystyle=(\widehat{T(F_{p}^{+})},\mathrm{H}^{n}(J_{B}(\Pi_{c}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{\operatorname{la}}))^{\prime},\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}^{S}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}},\psi_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}},n})

\displaystyle D_{\partial}^{n}(\widetilde{G})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}

\displaystyle=(\widehat{T(F_{p}^{+})},\mathrm{H}^{n}(J_{B}(\Pi_{\partial}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{\operatorname{la}}))^{\prime},\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}^{S}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}},\psi_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}},n})

where the
\psi
’s are the induced continuous Hecke actions on the modules in question, which exist essentially by transport of structure and localizing. From these data we define eigenvarieties
\mathcal{E}^{n}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}},

\mathcal{E}^{n}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}},\mathcal{E}_{c}^{n}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}},\mathcal{E}_{\partial}^{n}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}
.

Remark 2.33
.

One might wonder if we could use the abstract Hecke algebras
\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}^{S}
and
{\mathbf{T}}^{S}
instead of the “big” Hecke algebras
\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}^{S}(\widetilde{K}^{p})
and
{\mathbf{T}}^{S}(K^{p})
. Indeed, we claim that this choice is irrelevant and the
\mathcal{O}(\widehat{T})
-algebras generated by the images of
\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}^{S}
and
{\mathbf{T}}^{S}
coincide with those coming from the big Hecke algebras. For simplicity, consider the case of
\psi_{\mathfrak{m},n}:{\mathbf{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}}^{S}(K^{p})\to\operatorname{End}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\widehat{T}}}(\mathcal{M})
, where
\mathcal{M}=\mathrm{H}^{n}(J_{B}(\Pi(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\operatorname{la}}))^{\prime}
. Let
\mathcal{L}
be the coherent sheaf of endomorphisms generated by the image of
\psi_{\mathfrak{m},n}
. By construction, the natural map
{\mathbf{T}}^{S}\to{\mathbf{T}}^{S}(K^{p})
has dense image. It also suffices to work locally and thus consider a small enough neighborhood
(\Omega\times U_{\leq h})\subset\widehat{Y}
, so that
\mathcal{M}(\Omega\times U_{\leq h})
is finitely generated over
\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
by
Proposition

2.18
. By definition,
\mathcal{L}
is the sheaf constructed by the image of the map
\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}^{S}(\widetilde{K}^{p})\times\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{Y}}\to\operatorname{End}(\mathcal{M})
, and so the image of
{\mathbf{T}}^{S}(\widetilde{K}^{p})\times\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{Y}}(\Omega\times U_{\leq h})\to\mathcal{L}(\Omega\times U_{\leq h})
is surjective, and
\mathcal{L}(\Omega\times U_{\leq h})
is finitely generated over
\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
. The image of
{\mathbf{T}}^{S}\times\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{Y}}(U_{\leq h}\otimes\Omega)
is an
\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
-submodule, and thus automatically closed in
\mathcal{L}(\Omega\times U_{\leq h})
, so is equal to the image of its closure, as required.

However, our eigenvariety data with the big Hecke algebras provides a cleaner connection to Galois deformation spaces. First, recall that given any maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}\subset{\mathbf{T}}^{S}(K^{p})
, Scholze
[
Sch15
]
has constructed the associated mod
p
Galois representation
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}:\operatorname{Gal}_{F}\to\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p})
. In other words,
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is a continuous, semisimple representation unramified outside a finite set of places
S
, such that if
\nu\notin S
, then the characteristic polynomial of
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\operatorname{Frob}_{\nu})
is equal to
P_{\nu}(X)\mod\mathfrak{m}
. We say
\mathfrak{m}
is
non-Eisenstein
if
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is absolutely irreducible. We then let
R_{\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}}
be the universal deformation ring (which exists when
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is irreducible) and
R_{\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}}^{\square}
the associated framed deformation ring (which always exists). Let
X(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})
and
X^{\square}(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})
be the associated rigid spaces.
An essentially immediate consequence of Scholze’s construction of Galois representations associated to torsion classes then is the following.

Lemma 2.34
.

Suppose
\mathfrak{m}\subset{\mathbf{T}}^{S}(K^{p})
is a non-Eisenstein maximal ideal. Then
there is a continuous
n
-dimensional Galois representation

\rho:\operatorname{Gal}_{F}\to\operatorname{GL}_{n}({\mathbf{T}}^{S}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}/I)

for
I\subset{\mathbf{T}}^{S}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}
a nilpotent ideal, which is unramified outside
S

such that for all
\nu\notin S
, the characteristic polynomial of
\rho(\operatorname{Frob}_{\nu})
equals the image of
P_{\nu}(X)
in
{\mathbf{T}}^{S}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}/I[X]
.

As a result, there is a closed embedding
\mathcal{E}^{i}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}\hookrightarrow X^{\square}(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})\times\widehat{T_{n}(F_{p})}
(alternatively into
X(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})\times\widehat{T_{n}(F_{p})}
).

Proof.

As mentioned in
[
GN22
, Remark 3.3.4]
, we have a continuous
n
-dimensional representation valued in
{\mathbf{T}}^{S}(K^{p})/I
with
I
a nilpotent ideal with
I^{4}=0
. Thus, there also exists a representation in the reduction.

Now consider the Hecke action
\psi_{n,\mathfrak{m}}:{\mathbf{T}}^{S}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}\to\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{O}(\widehat{T_{n}(F_{p})})}(\mathrm{H}^{n}(J_{B}(\Pi(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\operatorname{la}})))^{\prime}
.
Let
\mathcal{A}_{i}
be the reduced
\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{T}}
-algebra generated by
\operatorname{im}(\psi_{i,\mathfrak{m}})
. We thus have an algebra homomorphism
R_{\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}}\to({\mathbf{T}}^{S}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}})^{\operatorname{red}}\to\mathcal{A}_{i}
. Passing to spectra, we have a natural map
\mathcal{E}^{i}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}\hookrightarrow(\operatorname{Spf}({\mathbf{T}}^{S}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\operatorname{red}}))^{\operatorname{rig}}\times\widehat{T}\to(\operatorname{Spf}R_{\overline{\rho}_{m}})^{\operatorname{rig}}\times\widehat{T}
.
∎

We need to shift
\widehat{T_{n}(F_{p})}
to match the weights of Galois representations. Let
j_{n}:\widehat{T_{n}(F_{p})}\to\widehat{T_{n}(F_{p})}
be the map defined by

j_{n}(\chi)_{\nu}:=\chi_{\nu}\cdot(1,\varepsilon_{\operatorname{cyc}}^{-1}\circ\operatorname{Art}_{F_{\nu}},\dots,\varepsilon_{\operatorname{cyc}}^{1-n}\circ\operatorname{Art}_{F_{\nu}})

For notational convenience, in the sequel we will write
\rho_{\operatorname{cyc}}^{G}:T_{n}(F_{p})\to\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}^{\times}
to denote the character
(\rho_{\operatorname{cyc}}^{G})_{\nu}:=(1,\varepsilon_{\operatorname{cyc}}^{-1}\circ\operatorname{Art}_{F_{\nu}},\dots,\varepsilon_{\operatorname{cyc}}^{-1}\circ\operatorname{Art}_{F_{\nu}})
. Similarly, we define
\rho_{\operatorname{cyc}}^{\widetilde{G}}:T(F_{p}^{+})\to\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}^{\times}
defined by the formula
(\rho_{\operatorname{cyc}}^{\widetilde{G}})_{\overline{\nu}}=(1,\varepsilon_{\operatorname{cyc}}^{-1}\circ\operatorname{Art}_{F_{\nu}},\dots,\varepsilon_{\operatorname{cyc}}^{1-2n}\circ\operatorname{Art}_{F_{\nu}})
. Moreover, given a character
\delta:T_{n}(F_{p})\to\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}^{\times}
, we will write the product
\delta\cdot\rho_{\operatorname{cyc}}^{G}
(respectively,
\delta\cdot\rho_{\operatorname{cyc}}^{\widetilde{G}}
) as
\delta+\rho_{\operatorname{cyc}}^{G}
(respectively,
\rho+\rho_{\operatorname{cyc}}^{\widetilde{G}}
). In particular,
j_{n}(\delta):=\delta+\rho_{\operatorname{cyc}}^{\widetilde{G}}
, and likewise if we view
\delta:T_{n}(F_{p})\to\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}^{\times}
as a character of
T(F_{p}^{+})
, we define
j_{2n}(\delta):=\delta+\rho_{\operatorname{cyc}}^{\widetilde{G}}
.

In essence, the goal of the paper is to constrain the image of these maps to the deformation space

\iota_{\mathfrak{m}}^{i}:\mathcal{E}^{i}(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}\hookrightarrow X^{\square}(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}})\times\widehat{T_{n}(F_{p})},

sending
(x,\delta)\mapsto(\rho_{x},j_{n}(\delta))
.

2.35
Reduction to a parabolic induction

The relation between the completed cohomology of
G=\operatorname{Res}_{F/F^{+}}\operatorname{GL}_{n}
and the boundary (completed) cohomology of
\widetilde{G}
is packaged nicely as follows:

Proposition 2.36
.

If
\mathfrak{m}\subset{\mathbf{T}}^{S}(K^{p})
is non–Eisenstein and
\widetilde{K}\subset\widetilde{G}({\mathbb{A}}_{F^{+}}^{\infty})
is a good subgroup decomposed with respect to
\overline{P},
then in the derived category
D^{b}(\mathsf{Ban}_{L}^{\operatorname{ad}}(\widetilde{G}_{0})
we have

\operatorname{ct}-\operatorname{Ind}_{\overline{P}_{0}}^{\widetilde{G}_{0}}\Pi(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}
is a
\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}^{S}
-equivariant direct summand of
\Pi_{\partial}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}
.

Proof.

This is
[
Fu22b
, Proposition 7.1]
, which in turn is deduced from the calculation
[
ACC+23
, Theorem 5.4.1]
.
∎

For the sake of taking derived functors we want a complex of
injective

\widetilde{G}_{0}
-representations representing
\operatorname{ct}-\operatorname{Ind}_{\overline{P}_{0}}^{\widetilde{G}_{0}}\Pi(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}
. Indeed, let
\Pi_{\overline{P}}(\widetilde{K}^{p}):=\Pi(\widetilde{K}^{p}\cap\overline{P}({\mathbb{A}}_{F^{+}}^{\infty,p}))
be a complex of injective admissible
\overline{P}_{0}
-representations computing the completed cohomology for the (opposite) Siegel parabolic subgroup
\overline{P}
. Recall that
X_{\widetilde{K}_{\overline{P}}}^{\overline{P}}\to X_{K}^{G}
has the structure of a torus bundle. Then (as in
[
ACC+23
, (5.4.2)]
and comments thereafter) there is a
\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}^{S}
-equivariant map of complexes of
\overline{P}_{0}
-representations
\Pi(K^{p})\to\Pi_{\overline{P}}(\widetilde{K}^{p})
, where the
\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}^{S}
-action on the left is induced by the Satake transfer
\mathcal{S}:\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}^{S}\to{\mathbf{T}}^{S}
. In fact, letting
A_{\widetilde{G}}^{\bullet}
be the complex of Banach spaces which makes
\Pi(K^{p})
a
G
-extension, and making the analogous construction
A_{\overline{P}}^{\bullet}
for
\overline{P}
, pullback induces a map
\pi_{G}^{\ast}:A_{\widetilde{G}}^{\bullet}\to A_{\overline{P}}^{\bullet}
, and the map
\Pi(K^{p})\to\Pi_{\overline{P}}(\widetilde{K}^{p})
can be chosen to be compatible with
\pi_{G}^{\ast}
. Since the completed cohomology of a unipotent subgroup
\overline{N}
is concentrated in degree
0
(in particular, see the second half of
[
ACC+23
, Proof of Theorem 5.4.1]
), this map is in fact a quasi-isomorphism. Moreover by passing to the dual side and again invoking
[
Wei94
, Corollary 10.4.7]
, we have a
G_{0}
-equivariant homotopy inverse
\Pi_{\overline{P}}(\widetilde{K}^{p})\to\operatorname{Inf}(\Pi(K^{p}))
. This map is in fact also continuous, since any map
{\mathcal{O}}_{L}[[G_{0}]]
-linear map
{\mathcal{O}}_{L}[[G_{0}]]\to{\mathcal{O}}_{L}[[\overline{P}_{0}]]
is automatically continuous.

Moreover for any maximal ideal
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}\subset\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}^{S}(\widetilde{K}^{p})
we can also run the same procedure as in
Section

2.12.1
to get a complex
\Pi_{\overline{P}}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}
of injective admission
\overline{P}_{0}
-representations equipped with a
\overline{P}
-extension.
Thus,
\operatorname{ct}-\operatorname{Inf}_{\overline{P}_{0}}^{\widetilde{G}_{0}}\Pi_{\overline{P}}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}
is a complex of injective
G_{0}
-representations representing
\operatorname{ct}-\operatorname{Ind}_{\overline{P}_{0}}^{\widetilde{G}_{0}}\Pi(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}
. Moreover, by
[
Fu22b
, Proposition 3.13]
we also have homotopy equivalences

\operatorname{ct}-\operatorname{Ind}_{\overline{P}_{0}}^{\widetilde{G}_{0}}\Pi_{\overline{P}}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}\to\operatorname{ct}-\operatorname{Ind}_{\overline{P}}^{\widetilde{G}_{p}}A_{\overline{P},\mathfrak{m}}^{\bullet}

As a result,
\operatorname{ct}-\operatorname{Ind}_{\overline{P}_{0}}^{\widetilde{G}_{0}}\Pi_{\overline{P}}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}
is a complex of injective admissible Banach
\widetilde{G}_{0}
-representations, equipped with a
\widetilde{G}_{p}
-extension, whose cohomology computes
\operatorname{ct}-\operatorname{Ind}_{\overline{P}}^{\widetilde{G}}\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}^{\ast}(K^{p},L)_{\mathfrak{m}}
. Then using
[
Fu22b
, Theorem 3.6]
, along with our previously defined formulae, we may consider the associated Jacquet functors

J_{B}(\operatorname{Ind}_{\overline{P}_{0}}^{\widetilde{G}_{0}}\Pi_{\overline{P}}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{\operatorname{la}})=(\operatorname{Ind}_{\overline{P}_{0}}^{\widetilde{G}_{0}}\Pi_{\overline{P}}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{\operatorname{la}})_{\operatorname{fs}}^{N_{0}}

with respect to the
\widetilde{G}
-extension provided. We can then likewise define an eigenvariety datum
D_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\operatorname{Ind}}(G)=(\widehat{T(F_{p}^{+})},\mathrm{H}^{n}(J_{B}(\operatorname{Ind}_{\overline{P}_{0}}^{\widetilde{G}_{0}}(\Pi(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\operatorname{la}})))^{\prime},\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}^{S}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}},\psi_{\operatorname{Ind},\mathfrak{m}})
and consider an associated eigenvariety
\mathcal{E}_{\operatorname{Ind}}^{n}(K^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}
.

In the case that
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}\subset\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}^{S}(\widetilde{K}^{p})
is so that
\overline{\rho}_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}
is decomposed generic as defined in
Definition

5.2
, by
Lemma

2.24
we have an injective (resp. surjective) map of essentially admissible representations

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{d-1}(J_{B}(\operatorname{Ind}_{\overline{P}_{0}}^{\widetilde{G}_{0}}\Pi_{\overline{P}}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{\operatorname{la}}))

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{d}(J_{B}(\Pi_{c}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{\operatorname{la}}))

(10)

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{d}(J_{B}(\Pi(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{\operatorname{la}}))

\displaystyle\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{d}(J_{B}(\operatorname{Ind}_{\overline{P}_{0}}^{\widetilde{G}_{0}}\Pi_{\overline{P}}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{\operatorname{la}})).

(11)

Implicitly, here we are using that the direct summand
\mathrm{H}^{i}(J_{B}(\operatorname{Ind}_{\overline{P}_{0}}^{\widetilde{G}_{0}}\Pi_{\overline{P}}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\operatorname{la}}))\to\mathrm{H}^{i}(J_{B}(\Pi_{\partial}(\widetilde{K}^{p})_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{\operatorname{la}}))
is
T(F_{p}^{+})
-equivariant, which follows from the fact that
[
ACC+23
, Theorem 5.4.1]
in fact gives the original direct summand is
\widetilde{G}(F_{p}^{+})
-equivariant (up to homotopy).

So to gain an understanding of Jacquet functors for
\Pi(K^{p})_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\operatorname{la}}
, we will start by understanding the Jacquet functors of a parabolic induction.

3
Locally analytic parabolic inductions

In this section, we make a more detailed study of parabolic inductions. We introduce the following notation.
Let
F/{\mathbf{Q}}
be a number field and recall
F_{p}:=F\otimes_{{\mathbf{Q}}}{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}=\prod_{\nu\in S_{p}(F)}F_{\nu}
. Let
L/{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
be a finite extension.

Let
\widetilde{G}=\widetilde{G}(F_{p})
be the
F_{p}
-points of a split connected reductive group over
F_{p}
,
B=B(F_{p})=T(F_{p})N(F_{p})
a Borel with choice of split maximal torus
T\subset B
and
P=P(F_{p})=G(F_{p})U(F_{p})\supset B
a parabolic subgroup containing
B
with Levi
G
and unipotent radical
U
. Let
\overline{P}:=\overline{P}(F_{p})
be the corresponding opposite parabolic. Even though we will only need to take
\widetilde{G}=\operatorname{GL}_{2n}(F_{p})
and
P=G(F_{p})U(F_{p})
the (opposite) Siegel parabolic, the following section applies to any split reductive group.

Let
\pi
be a locally analytic representation of
G(F_{p})
on a compact type vector space over
L
.
We are interested in studying locally analytic parabolic inductions

\operatorname{Ind}_{\overline{P}}^{\widetilde{G}}\pi:=\{f\in C^{\rm{la}}(\widetilde{G},\pi):f(pg)=\pi(p)f(g)\text{ for all }p\in\overline{P},g\in\widetilde{G}\}.

(12)

as a representation of the Borel
B
. Since
\overline{P}\backslash\widetilde{G}
is compact,
\operatorname{Ind}_{\overline{P}}^{\widetilde{G}}\pi
is again a compact type
L
-vector space.

Let
W_{\overline{P}}^{r}:=W_{G}\backslash W_{\widetilde{G}}
be the relative Weyl set. Then a generalized Bruhat decomposition gives a stratification into locally closed subsets

\widetilde{G}(F_{p})=\bigsqcup_{w\in W_{\overline{P}}^{r}}\overline{P}(F_{p})wB(F_{p})=\bigsqcup_{w\in W_{\overline{P}}^{r}}\overline{P}(F_{p})wN(F_{p}),

so that in particular
W_{\overline{P}}^{r}:=\overline{P}(F_{p})\backslash\widetilde{G}(F_{p})/B(F_{p})
, which additionally satisfies that
\overline{P}N\subset G
is Zariski open and dense, and if
C_{w}:=\overline{P}(F_{p})wN(F_{p})
, then the closure
\overline{C}_{w}=\sqcup_{w^{\prime}:\ell(w^{\prime})\leq\ell(w)}C_{w^{\prime}}
, where
\ell(w)
is understood to be the minimal length of a representative element in
W_{\widetilde{G}}
of the coset of
w
.

Let
W^{\overline{P}}
be a collection of minimal length representatives for each class in
W_{\overline{P}}^{r}
. Now fix an ordering
\{w_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\#{W_{\overline{P}}^{r}}}=W^{\overline{P}}
such that if
i\leq j
then
\ell(w_{i})\leq\ell(w_{j})
. Then define for all
k\geq 1,

\widetilde{G}_{k}:=\bigsqcup_{1\leq i\leq k}\overline{P}(F_{p})w_{i}N(F_{p}),

which is naturally an open and dense subset of
\widetilde{G}(F_{p})
.
We first note that we can write coordinates for an open cell.

Lemma 3.1
.

We have an inclusion
\overline{P}wN\subset\overline{P}Uw
. Moreover we have an isomorphism of locally
{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
-analytic manifolds

\overline{P}wN\times(w^{-1}Uw\cap\overline{N})\simeq\overline{P}Uw

induced by the product
(x,y)\mapsto x\cdot y
.

Proof.

This inclusion is a general fact about reductive groups. Let
\Phi^{+}
(resp.
\Phi^{-}
) be the set of positive (resp. negative) roots with respect to
(B,T)
. Moreover let
\Phi_{U}^{+}
be the set of roots in the unipotent radical
U\subset P
. Now first note that it suffices to show that
wNw^{-1}\subset\overline{P}N=\overline{P}U
. The key fact is that as algebraic varieties (or schemes) over
F_{p}
, we have
N=\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}}U_{\alpha}
, where
U_{\alpha}
is the root group associated with a positive root
\alpha
, and the product can be taken
in any order
. As a result, we may rewrite

wNw^{-1}=\prod_{\alpha\in w(\Phi^{+})w^{-1}}U_{\alpha}=\left(\prod_{\alpha\in w(\Phi^{+})w^{-1}\cap\Phi^{-}}U_{\alpha}\right)\cdot\left(\prod_{\beta\in w(\Phi^{+})w^{-1}\cap\Phi^{+}}U_{\beta}\right)\subseteq\overline{P}\cdot N,

(13)

proving the first part. For the second part, we note first that
\overline{P}Uw\simeq\overline{P}\times Uw
as algebraic varieties.
Likewise, (
13
) implies that
\overline{P}wN\simeq\overline{P}\times\left(\prod_{\beta\in w(\Phi^{+})w^{-1}\cap\Phi_{U}^{+}}U_{\alpha}\right)w
. For the result, it then suffices to show the
U\cap w\overline{N}w^{-1}\simeq\prod_{\alpha\in w\Phi^{-}w^{-1}\cap\Phi_{U}^{+}}U_{\alpha}
, and that
\Phi_{U}^{+}\cap w\Phi^{-}w^{-1}\sqcup w\Phi^{-}w^{-1}\cap\Phi_{U}^{+}=\Phi_{U}^{+}
, so that all the relevant roots are represented exactly once.
∎

Lemma

3.1
gives us a convenient way to discuss open neighborhoods of Bruhat strata. Namely, if
U_{\underline{x}}\subset w^{-1}Uw\cap\overline{N}
is a compact open subset, then the subset
U_{w,\underline{x}}:=\overline{P}wN\times\left(U_{\underline{x}}\right)\subset\overline{P}Uw
is naturally an open set containing the Bruhat stratum
C_{w}
.
We recall that any function
f\in\operatorname{Ind}_{\overline{P}}^{\widetilde{G}}\pi
has a well-defined support
\operatorname{supp}f\subseteq\overline{P}\backslash G
, given by the locus on which the germ of
f
is nonzero. By definition, the support is a compact and open subset of the quotient
\overline{P}\backslash\widetilde{G}
.

Now, for any open subset
\Omega\subset\overline{P}\backslash\widetilde{G}
, we define
(\operatorname{Ind}_{\overline{P}}^{G}\pi)(\Omega)
to be the subspace of
f
such that
\operatorname{supp}f\subseteq\Omega
. For shorthand we also write
I_{\Omega}\pi:=(\operatorname{Ind}_{\overline{P}}^{G}\pi)(\Omega)
.

We now make our key definitions for the Bruhat filtration on locally analytic inductions:

Definition 3.2
.

For any
k\geq 0
and
w\in W_{\overline{P}}^{r}
, define the functors
I_{\leq k},I_{w}:\operatorname{Rep}_{\rm{la.c}}(P)\to\operatorname{Rep}(B)
by

I_{\leq k}(\pi):=(\operatorname{Ind}_{\overline{P}}^{\widetilde{G}}\pi)(\widetilde{G}_{k}),\quad

I_{w}(\pi)=\varinjlim(\operatorname{Ind}_{\overline{P}}^{\widetilde{G}}\pi)(U_{w,\underline{x}}),

with transition maps
\operatorname{Ind}_{\overline{P}}^{\widetilde{G}}V(U_{w,\underline{x}})\to\operatorname{Ind}_{\overline{P}}^{\widetilde{G}}V(U_{w,\underline{y}})
is the map
f\mapsto f\cdot\mathbf{1}_{U_{w,\underline{y}}}
.

Some remarks are in hand. In these definitions,
I_{\leq k}\pi\subset\operatorname{Ind}_{\overline{P}}^{\widetilde{G}}\pi
is naturally a closed subspace in
\operatorname{Rep}_{\rm{la}.c}(B)
.
For
I_{w}
, note that the transition maps are well-defined since if
\operatorname{supp}f\subset U_{w,\underline{x}}
is a compact open subset then
(\operatorname{supp}f)\cap U_{w,\underline{y}}
remains compact open, since
\left(U_{\underline{y}}\cap w\overline{U}w^{-1}\right)\subset\left(U_{\underline{x}}\cap w\overline{U}w^{-1}\right)
, and so
(\operatorname{supp}f)\cap U_{w,\underline{y}}\subset\operatorname{supp}f
is closed, thus compact. We then endow
I_{w}
with the locally convex inductive limit topology.

Remark 3.3
.

As in
[
ACC+23
]
, we could define a certain sub-functor
I_{w}^{\circ}\pi\subset I_{w}\pi
defined by germs of functions
f
such that some equivalent function satisfies
\operatorname{supp}f_{\underline{x}_{0}}\cap PwN\subseteq PwN_{0}
. A key point in
[
ACC+23
, §5.3]
is that in the smooth mod
p
case, the ordinary parts of
I_{w}^{\circ}
and
I_{w}
coincide, and the ordinary parts of
I_{w}^{\circ}
is particularly simple and related to
G
. In our setting, we would expect that the Jacquet functors of
I_{w}^{\circ}
and
I_{w}
agree on
T
-
eigenspaces
, but
I_{w}^{\circ}
remains a somewhat complicated object. As our argument does not use this subfunctor, we do not study it.

A variant of
[
Eme07
, Lemma 1.4.10]
implies that
I_{w}\pi
is also of compact type if
\pi\in\operatorname{Rep}_{\rm{la}.c}(\overline{P})
is of compact type.
It will be convenient to note the following alternative description:

Lemma 3.4
.

If
\pi\in\operatorname{Rep}_{\rm{la}.c}(\overline{P})
then we have a
B
-equivariant isomorphism of topological vector spaces

I_{\leq k}\pi\simeq\varinjlim_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\subset\overline{P}\backslash\overline{P}\widetilde{G}_{k}\\
\text{compact open}\end{subarray}}(\operatorname{Ind}_{\overline{P}}^{\widetilde{G}}\pi)(U).

Proof.

Since any function
f\in\operatorname{Ind}_{\overline{P}}^{G}\pi
has compact support we indeed have a continuous bijective map

\varinjlim_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\subset\overline{P}\backslash\overline{P}\widetilde{G}_{k}\\
\text{compact open}\end{subarray}}(\operatorname{Ind}_{\overline{P}}^{\widetilde{G}}\pi)(U)\to\operatorname{Ind}_{\overline{P}}^{\widetilde{G}}\pi(\widetilde{G}_{k})=I_{\leq k}\pi.

Moreover, both spaces are of compact type (the left side being compact type because for
U\subsetneq V\subset\overline{P}\backslash\widetilde{G}
compact open, the inclusion map
\operatorname{Ind}_{\overline{P}}^{\widetilde{G}}\pi(U)\to\operatorname{Ind}_{\overline{P}}^{\widetilde{G}}\pi(V)
is compact, so by the open mapping theorem we have a topological isomorphism.
∎

Lemma 3.5
.

If
0\to U\to V\to W\to 0
is a strict exact sequence of
L
-vector spaces of compact type and
X
any compact locally
L
-analytic manifold, the sequence
0\to\mathcal{C}^{\rm{la}}(X,U)\to\mathcal{C}^{\rm{la}}(X,V)\to\mathcal{C}^{\rm{la}}(X,W)\to 0
is also strict exact.

Proof.

The maps in the stipulated sequence are clearly continuous
L
-linear and all the spaces are of compact type by
[
Eme17
, 2.1.28]
, and the map
\mathcal{C}^{\rm{la}}(X,U)\to\mathcal{C}^{\rm{la}}(X,V)
is a closed embedding by
[
Eme17
, 2.1.29]
, so by the open mapping theorem it suffices to show that the sequence is exact as abstract
L
-vector spaces.

Note that when
X
is compact and
M\simeq\varinjlim_{n}M_{n}
is of compact type, we have a topological isomorphism
\mathcal{C}^{\rm{la}}(X,M)\simeq\varinjlim_{\{X_{i}\}}\prod_{i}\varinjlim_{n}\mathcal{C}^{\rm{an}}(\mathbb{X}_{i},L)\widehat{\otimes}_{L}M_{n}
where the
\{X_{i}\}
runs over disjoint partitions of
X
by analytic charts (see
[
Eme17
, p. 38]
). Since
X
is compact, we can take each of these products to be finite. Moreover by
[
Eme17
, 2.1.10]
that we have a topological isomorphism
\mathcal{C}^{\rm{an}}(\mathbb{X}_{i},L)\simeq c_{0}({\mathbf{N}},L)
, where
c_{0}({\mathbf{N}},L)\subseteq\mathcal{C}({\mathbf{N}}\cup\{\infty\},L)
is the subspace of continuous functions on the one-point compactification of
{\mathbf{N}}
which vanish at
\infty
. Since the
M_{n}
are Banach (in particular, Fréchet) we have
\mathcal{C}({\mathbf{N}}\cup\{\infty\},L)\widehat{\otimes}_{L}M_{n}\simeq\mathcal{C}({\mathbf{N}}\cup\{\infty\},M_{n})
, and this identification will clearly match up
